Scattering diagrams, stability conditions, and coherent sheaves on ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} Mathematics Subject Classification: 14N35
Pierrick Bousseau

Abstract
. We show that a purely algebraic structure, a two-dimensional scattering diagram, describes a large part of the wall-crossing behavior of moduli spaces of Bridgeland semistable objects in the derived category of coherent sheaves on ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} . This gives a new algorithm computing the Hodge numbers of the intersection cohomology of the classical moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves on ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} , or equivalently the refined Donaldson-Thomas invariants for compactly supported sheaves on local ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} . As applications, we prove that the intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves on ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} is Hodge-Tate, and we give the first non-trivial numerical checks of the general χ \chi -independence conjecture for refined Donaldson-Thomas invariants of one-dimensional sheaves on local ℙ 2 \mathbb{P}^{2} .



0.
Introduction

0.1.
Overview

The concept of scattering diagram comes from the work of
Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS06
]

and Gross-Siebert
[
GS11
]
in mirror symmetry. In this context, a scattering diagram is an algebraic structure which is supposed to encode the behavior of holomorphic discs with boundary
on torus fibers of the Strominger-Yau-Zaslow fibration
[
SYZ96
]
. Essentially the same algebraic structure appears in an a priori completely different setting: the wall-crossing behavior of Donaldson-Thomas counts of semistable objects in a Calabi-Yau triangulated category of dimension
3
, upon variation of the stability condition
[
KS08
,
JS12
,
KS14
]
.
Some precise connection between scattering diagrams and spaces of stability conditions
for quivers with potential has been established by Bridgeland
[
Bri17
]
, recently followed by Cheung-Mandel
[
CM19
]
.

The aim of the present paper is to explore further this connection between stability conditions and scattering diagrams in a specific geometric example.
We consider the complex projective plane

\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the space of Bridgeland stability conditions

\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

[
Bri07
]
on the bounded derived category
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
The space
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is a complex manifold of complex dimension
3
. We focus on a
particular subset
U
of
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of complex dimension
1
. Using as input the intersection cohomology of the moduli spaces of semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
at various points of
U
, we
construct a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
on
U
. On the other hand, we give some purely algorithmic definition of another scattering diagram

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
on
U
.

Our main result is that these two scattering diagrams coincide.

Theorem 0.1.1

(=Theorem
5.2.1
)
.

We have

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})\,.

We stress that the left-hand side
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
encodes some complicated geometry of the moduli spaces
of semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, whereas the right-hand side
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is completely algorithmic and can be easily implemented on a computer.

As the notion of Gieseker stability can be recovered as a limiting case of Bridgeland stability condition, Theorem
0.1.1

gives a new algorithm to compute intersection cohomology of the classical
[
HL10
]
moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Using this algorithm, we prove that the intersection cohomology of the moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is concentrated in Hodge bidegrees
(p,p)
(Theorem
0.4.1
), and we make the first non-trivial numerical checks of the general

\chi
-independence conjecture for intersection cohomology of Gieseker semistable one-dimensional sheaves (Conjecture
0.4.3
-Theorem
0.4.5
).

We have phrased our main result in terms of the derived category of coherent sheaves on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
. One motivation for this choice is that moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are classical objects of algebraic geometry and the study of their intersection cohomology is an interesting topic on its own. Nevertheless, an alternative
more modern point of view would be to consider Theorem
0.1.1
as a statement about
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, the non-compact Calabi-Yau 3-fold total space of the canonical line bundle of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, also known as local
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Indeed,
U
can also be viewed as a subspace of the space of Bridgeland stability conditions of the category
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
of coherent sheaves on
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

set-theoretically supported on the zero-section, and the intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
involved in the definition of
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
coincides with the refined Donaldson-Thomas invariants of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.
In particular, the combinatorial understanding of
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

provided by Theorem
0.1.1
is an expression of the Kontsevich-Soibelman wall-crossing formula
[
KS08
]
for Donaldson-Thomas invariants of Calabi-Yau 3-folds.

In the follow-up paper
[
Bou19b
]
, we will combine Theorem
0.1.1
with the main result of
Gabele
[
Gab19
]
to prove
N. Takahashi’s conjecture

[
Tak01
,
CvGKT18b
]
on genus-
0
Gromov-Witten theory with maximal tangency of the pair
(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
, where
E
is a smooth cubic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
We
will also establish in
[
Bou19b
]
a new
sheaves/Gromov–Witten correspondence, relating Betti numbers of moduli spaces of one-dimensional Gieseker semistable sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, or equivalently refined genus-
0
Gopakumar–Vafa invariants of local
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
with higher-genus maximal contact Gromov–Witten theory of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
.
In combination with the work
[
BFGW20
]
on the Gromov-Witten side,
this will give the first mathematical proof of a non-trivial example of the general structure properties (finite generation, quasimodularity, holomorphic anomaly equation)
expected from string theory for the refined topological string on Calabi-Yau 3-folds

[
HK12
]
.

The rest of the introduction is organized as follows.
In
section

0.2
we give a more detailed description of the objects

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

involved in the statement of Theorem

0.1.1
.
In
section

0.3
we briefly describe the technical tools used in the proof of Theorem
0.1.1
.
In
section

0.4

we state precisely our results on moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Finally, we discuss connections with related works in
section

0.5
.

0.2.
Description of the scattering diagrams
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

0.2.1.
Scattering diagrams

Both
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
and

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
are scattering diagrams on
U
for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
. Here,
U
is the open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by

U\coloneq\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|x^{2}+2y>0\}\,,

and
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
the
\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}((uv)^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}})z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,,

where

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\langle(a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle\coloneq 3(a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime})\,.

For the purposes of the present paper, a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
on
U
for

\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
is a collection of rays
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
, where:

•

H_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\mathfrak{d}}}
for some

m_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
called the class of the ray

\mathfrak{d}
.

•

|\mathfrak{d}|
is an oriented line segment or half-line contained in
U
, of direction
-m_{\mathfrak{d}}
, and called the support of the ray

\mathfrak{d}
.

To every ray
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
of scattering diagram, we attach an
automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
given by

\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq\exp([H_{\mathfrak{d}},-])\,.

In fact, in order to make sense of the power series definition of the exponential, one needs to work with various completions of
\mathfrak{g}
. In this introduction, we ignore this issue and we refer to
section

1
for details.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
on
U
for
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
is consistent if, for every
\sigma\in U
, the composition of the automorphisms
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\epsilon_{\mathfrak{d}}(\sigma)}
, taken over all the rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
\mathfrak{D}
passing through

\sigma
and in the anticlockwise order around
\sigma
, is the identity automorphism. Here, we set
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{d}}(\sigma)=\pm 1
depending if the orientation of
\mathfrak{d}
points towards
\sigma
or not.

An elementary but important fact going back to Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS06
]
gives a systematic way to construct consistent scattering diagrams.
Let us start with a scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}
. Then
\mathfrak{D}
is not necessarily consistent: there can exist some
\sigma\in U
such that the composition of
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\epsilon_{\mathfrak{d}}(\sigma)}
around
\sigma
is not the identity.
Then, Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS06
]
proved that there is an essentially unique way to add rays starting at
\sigma
in order to form a new scattering diagram such that the composition of the automorphisms
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\epsilon_{\mathfrak{d}}(\sigma)}
around
\sigma
is now equal to the identity. The rays that need to be added are completely determined by the consistency condition and so by the Lie bracket of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
.
The new scattering diagram is now consistent around
\sigma
, but due to the newly added rays, there are now maybe new points
\sigma^{\prime}\in U
where consistency fails, and the construction needs to be iterated by successive additions of new rays. Dealing with the convergence issues of this potentially infinite process (as done carefully in
section

1
), we end up with a consistent scattering diagram.
In other words, starting with any consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, there is a canonical way to produce a consistent one, that we denote
S(\mathfrak{D})
. Each time rays of

\mathfrak{D}
intersect, new rays are added to guarantee the consistency and the process is iterated. This explains the scattering terminology: when rays meet, they ‘scatter’ and produce new rays in a completely algorithmic way.

0.2.2.
The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
is obtained by the consistent completion

\mathfrak{D}\mapsto S(\mathfrak{D})
described above for a specific choice of initial scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
which can be explicitly described. We first remark that the boundary of
U
is the parabola in

\mathbb{R}^{2}
of equation
x^{2}+2y=0
. The support of the rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
are the tangent lines to this parabola at the points

s_{n}\coloneq(n,-\frac{n^{2}}{2})
. More precisely, we denote
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
the oriented tangent half-line of direction
-m_{n}^{+}
, where
m_{n}^{+}=(-1,n)
, and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
the oriented tangent half-line of direction

-m_{n}^{-}
, where
m_{n}^{-}=(1,-n)
.
Then the rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
are given by
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{+}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|,H_{n,\ell}^{+})

and
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{-}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|,H_{n,\ell}^{-})
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\ell\geqslant 1
, where

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We refer to Figure
1
for a pictorial representation of the support of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
, and to Figure

2
for a pictorial representation of the support of some of the first steps of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Figure 1.
The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}

0.2.3.
The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is constructed in terms of the moduli spaces of Bridgeland semistable objects in the bounded derived category
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
We denote
\Gamma\coloneq\mathbb{Z}^{3}=K_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
and
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\in\Gamma
, where
r
stands for the rank,
d
for the degree, and
\chi
for the Euler characteristic of an object in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Recall from
[
Bri07
]
that every
Bridgeland stability condition
\sigma
on

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
comes with the data of an additive map

Z^{\sigma}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\,,

\gamma\mapsto Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\,,

called the central charge. The notion of stability specified by
\sigma
is determined by the relative phases of the central charges
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
.

Bridgeland
stability conditions for polarized surfaces, and so in particular for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, have been well studied
[
Bri08
,
ABCH13
,
BM11
]
.
In particular, it is known how to construct
an explicit half-space

\mathbb{H}\coloneq\{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|t>0\}
of stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. The central charge for the stability
(s,t)\in\mathbb{H}
is given by

Z_{\gamma}^{(s,t)}=-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2})r+ds+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi+i(d-sr)t\,.

The main idea to construct scattering diagrams from stability conditions is to consider loci of stability conditions
\sigma
indexed by
\gamma\in\Gamma
and defined by the condition that the central charge
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
remains of constant phase. In terms of the
(s,t)
coordinates on
\mathbb{H}
, these loci are parabola in

\mathbb{H}
and not straight lines.
Our main remark is that the map

\mathbb{H}\rightarrow U

(s,t)\mapsto(x,y)=(s,-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2}))

is a bijection, such that, for every

\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\in\Gamma
, the locus

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0

has equation

ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi=0\,,

in terms of
(x,y)\in U
, and so is a straight line in
U
.
From now on, we use this identification

\mathbb{H}\simeq U
to view
U
as a space of stability conditions on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
In other words, we obtained
U
from

\mathbb{H}
by defining on
\mathbb{H}
an integral affine structure such that the functions
\sigma\mapsto\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
become integral affine coordinates.
The elementary change of variables

(s,t)\mapsto(x,y)

gives a new perspective on the standard upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
of stability conditions and is the key condition which makes the appearance of a scattering diagram possible.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\sigma\in U
, we have a projective variety
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
parametrizing S-equivalence classes of
\sigma
-semistable objects of class
\gamma
in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. We refer to
section

2.3
-
section

2.4
for details. The projective varieties

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
are in general singular, due to the existence of strictly semistable objects. Nevertheless, the intersection cohomology groups
IH^{k}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma},\mathbb{Q})
behave as well as cohomology of a smooth projective variety
[
GM80
,
GM83
,
BBD82
]
, and in particular
are naturally pure Hodge structures of weight
k

[
Sai90
]
. We denote
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})
the corresponding Hodge numbers, which can be organized into some signed symmetrized intersection
Hodge polynomial

Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\coloneq(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}\sum_{p,q=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}(-1)^{p+q}Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})u^{p}v^{q}\in\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]\,.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

is then described as follows.
For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we consider the set
R_{\gamma}
of points
\sigma\in U

such that
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0
and such that there exists
\sigma
-semistable objects of class
\gamma
. It happens that
R_{\gamma}
is some half-line, contained in the straight line of equation
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0
. Denoting

m_{\gamma}\coloneq(r,-d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
we attach to every point
\sigma
of
R_{\gamma}
some generating series

H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
:

H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\coloneq\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma\,,\ell\geqslant 1\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\,.

As a function of
\sigma\in R_{\gamma}
, the moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
, and so the Hodge numbers

Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})
and the generating series
H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
are locally constant away from points where they jump in a discontinuous way. At such points, where
R_{\gamma}
crosses walls in the space of stability conditions, the notion of semistability for objects of class
\gamma
changes. These points of wall-crossing give a subdivision of
R_{\gamma}
into line segments
R_{\gamma,j}
. We attach
to each line segment
R_{\gamma,j}
the corresponding generating series
H_{\gamma,j}\coloneq H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
which is now independent of
\sigma\in R_{\gamma,j}
by construction.
By definition,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is the scattering diagram on
U
for

\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
whose rays are the

(R_{\gamma,j},H_{\gamma,j})
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and for all
j
.

0.2.4.
Terminology

It is worth pointing that we use the terminology ‘scattering diagram’ in a slightly extended sense:
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

is really a structure in the sense of

[
GS11
]
or a wall-crossing structure in the sense of
[
KS14
]
. In other words, it is an infinite collection of local scattering diagrams in the sense of

[
GS11
]
. Our terminology choices come from trying to avoid the potential confusion between the usages of the word ‘wall’ in the mirror symmetry context and in the stability conditions context.

One should also remark that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is really a quantum scattering diagram, in the sense that the generating series
H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
are used to construct automorphisms of quantum tori. In some classical limit, generating series
H_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
reduce to generating series of Euler characteristics of the intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of semistable objects.

Figure 2.
First steps of the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
. Figure due to Tim Gabele
[
Gab19
]
.

0.3.
Structure of the proof of the main result

The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
is defined starting with some simple initial scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
and then taking its consistent completion. The proof of Theorem
0.1.1
, that is, of the equality
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
, has correspondingly two parts.
We first show that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is consistent, and then that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
has in some sense the same initial data as

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
. Then, the result follows from some form of uniqueness of the consistent completion.

0.3.1.
Consistency from wall-crossing formula

The most non-trivial property about
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
that we have to prove is its consistency. This is equivalent to
some wall-crossing formula for the Hodge numbers of intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of semistable objects upon variation of the stability condition.

The key point is the relation between intersection cohomology and Donaldson-Thomas invariants, which goes back to Meinhardt-Reineke
[
MR17
]
.
This relation has been extended to Gieseker semistable sheaves on surfaces with negative canonical line bundles in

[
MM18
]
and to some abstract
framework for categories of homological one in
[
Mei15
]
.

The crucial input that will able us to use this class of techniques is a result of Li-Zhao
[
LZ19b
]
1
1

1

Li-Zhao first proved this result for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
[
LZ19a
]
by a quite complicated argument using the explicit description of exceptional collections on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
. They gave a more general and much simpler proof in
[
LZ19b
]
:
for every
\sigma\in U
and for every class
\gamma
, the stack of
\sigma
-semistable objects of class
\gamma
is smooth. More precisely,
the
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}
-group between two
\sigma
-semistable sheaves of class
\gamma
vanishes if

\gamma
is not the class of a zero dimensional sheaf. This vanishing is well-known and obvious for Gieseker semistable sheaves, but is not obvious at all if
\sigma
is a general stability condition in
U
, so we are really using the non-trivial content of
[
LZ19b
]
. Once we know this vanishing, then we can apply the machinery described in
[
Mei15
]

to get that indeed Hodge numbers of intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of semistable objects satisfy the wall-crossing formula of
Kontsevich-Soibelman

[
KS08
]

and Joyce-Song
[
JS12
]
.

Alternatively, we could consider the derived category
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
of coherent sheaves on the total space of the canonical line bundle
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-3)
of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
which are set-theoretically supported on the zero-section
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. As
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
is a Calabi-Yau triangulated category of dimension
3
, it is a natural place for Donaldson-Thomas theory and the wall-crossing formula.

In fact,
U

can also be viewed naturally in the space
\operatorname{Stab}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
of Bridgeland stability conditions on
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
.
Moreover, it follows
2
2

2

For example adapting the proof of

[
CMT18
, Proposition 3.1]
given for Gieseker semistable sheaves.
from the vanishing result of Li-Zhao that for every
\sigma\in U
,

\sigma
-semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
have cohomology sheaves scheme-theoretically supported on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and so coincide with

\sigma
-semistable objects in

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
3
3

3

It is not true at all outside
U
. The spaces
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
and

\operatorname{Stab}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
behave globally in very different ways.
Our discussion is only valid on
U
.
.
Therefore, the Hodge numbers of
intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of

\sigma
-semistable objects in

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
are really
(refined) Donaldson-Thomas invariants of

K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.

The conclusion is that we think conceptually about
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
but we work technically on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Refined Donaldson-Thomas theory in the context of general
Calabi-Yau 3-folds requires a discussion of orientation data, and we refer to
[
Shi18
]
for
a discussion precisely in the case of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
but thanks to the smoothness of the moduli stack of semistable objects,
we don’t have to go into these technical aspects of the general story.

0.3.2.
Initial data

The combinatorial definition of the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

involves some very simple initial data, and then some completely algorithmic completion which guarantees its consistency.
Once we know that the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is consistent, it is enough to show that it has in some sense the same initial data as
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{u,v}
in order to conclude the equality

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
which is the statement of Theorem
0.1.1
.

We will show that, from the point of view of

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
, the initial rays

\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{+}
of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
correspond respectively to the line bundles
\mathcal{O}(n)
and their shift

\mathcal{O}(n)[1]
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. They come out from
the points
s_{n}
in the boundary parabola of
U
where the central charge of
\mathcal{O}(n)
vanishes.
In order to prove that

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
has the same initial data as

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
, it is enough to show that, in the region in
U
near the boundary parabola,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
consists only of the rays associated to
\mathcal{O}(n)
and

\mathcal{O}(n)[1]
.

This comes from the fact that this region can be decomposed into triangles,
in correspondence with some exceptional collections of objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. A stability condition
in the interior of such triangle is equivalent to some
stability condition with quiver heart.
Using the fact that the class of an object in a quiver heart
is linear combination with nonnegative coefficients of the classes of the three simple objects,
we can show that no object has
\operatorname{Re}\,Z=0
inside each triangle,
and
so that the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
,
defined in terms of the condition

\operatorname{Re}\,Z=0
, is trivial in the interior of all the triangles.

We remark that in order to have such simple picture,
with initial data in correspondence with line bundles and determining everything
else by wall-crossing,
it is essential to restrict our attention to semistable objects with a fixed phase of the central charge, such as the semistable objects with
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0
entering in the definition of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
.
Indeed, there is no point in
U
where the set of all semistable objects is really simple,
and so the naive idea to find a point in
U
where the set of all semistable objects is simple,
and then to move to a more complicated point by wall-crossing, does not work. By considering the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
, we are only looking at semistable objects with
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0
, and according to the previous paragraph, there is a region in
U
where the set of such semistable objects is simple.

0.4.
Applications to moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves

For every
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
, let

M_{\gamma}
be the moduli space of S-equivalence classes of Gieseker semistable sheaves of class
\gamma
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. A reference for the classical topic of moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves is the book
[
HL10
]
. For every

\gamma\in\Gamma
,
M_{\gamma}
is a projective variety, singular in general.
Nevertheless, the intersection cohomology groups
IH^{k}(M_{\gamma},\mathbb{Q})

behave as well as cohomology of a smooth projective variety, and in particular
is naturally a pure Hodge structure of weight
k
. We denote
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})

the corresponding Hodge numbers and
Ib_{j}(M_{\gamma})
the corresponding Betti numbers.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{\gamma}
for
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
with
y
large enough.
Theorem
0.1.1
gives an algorithmic way to compute the intersection Hodge numbers
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})
, and so the intersection Hodge numbers
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})
. Using this algorithm, we prove the following result.

Theorem 0.4.1

(=Theorem
6.1.2
)
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})=0
if
p\neq q
.

If
\gamma
is primitive
then semistability coincides with stability,

M_{\gamma}
is smooth, intersection cohomology coincides with ordinary cohomology, and in this case Theorem
0.4.1
is classical
[
ESm93
,
Bea95
,
Mar07
]
. In general, under the extra assumption
r>0
, a different proof of Theorem
0.4.1
could be extracted from
[
MM18
]
.

The fact that the lines bundles
\mathcal{O}(n)
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, generate
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
goes back to Beilinson
[
Bei78
]
. The scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
gives an explicit way, at the level of intersection cohomology of
moduli spaces, to reconstruct Gieseker semistable sheaves from line bundles
by successive exact triangles in the derived category.
In particular, we obtain a decomposition indexed by trees of
the intersection cohomology of moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves,
which seems to be new. We refer to

section

6.3
for details.

We also prove an analogue of Theorem
0.4.1
involving the real algebraic geometry of the moduli spaces

M_{\gamma}
. We refer to
section

6.2
for details.

Theorem 0.4.2
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and for every
0\leqslant p\leqslant\dim M_{\gamma}
, the complex conjugation in

\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
acts as
(-1)^{p}

on
IH^{2p}(M_{\gamma},\mathbb{Q})
.

Our final results on moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves concern sheaves supported on curves, that is with
\gamma=(0,d,\chi)
and
d\geq 1
. The intersection Betti numbers
Ib_{j}(M_{(0,d,\chi)})
are refined Donaldson-Thomas invariants for one-dimensional sheaves on
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, and so can be viewed as refined genus-
0

Gopakumar-Vafa invariants of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
(see
[
Bou19b
]
for details).
Tensor product with
\mathcal{O}(1)
and Serre duality induce isomorphisms

M_{(0,d,\chi)}\simeq M_{(0,d,\chi+d)}
and
M_{(0,d,\chi)}\simeq M_{(0,d,-\chi)}
.
On the other hand, if
d\geq 3
and
\chi^{\prime}\neq\pm\chi\mod d
, then the algebraic varieties
M_{(0,d,\chi)}
and
M_{(0,d,\chi^{\prime})}
are not isomorphic by
[
Woo13
, Theorem 8.1]
. This makes the following conjecture particularly non-trivial.

Conjecture 0.4.3
.

For every fixed
d\geq 1
, the intersection Betti numbers
Ib_{j}(M_{d,\chi})
do not depend on
\chi
.

It is a general conjecture due to Joyce-Song
[
JS12
, Conjecture 6.20]

(see also
[
Tod12
, Conjecture 6.3]
) that (unrefined) Donaldson-Thomas invariants for one-dimensional sheaves on Calabi-Yau 3-folds should be
\chi
-independent.
This conjecture is known for
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
(see
[
CMT18
, Appendix A]

and
[
Bou19b
]
). Conjecture
0.4.3
is the refined version of the Joyce-Song conjecture.

Using Theorem
0.1.1
as an essential ingredient, we will prove in
[
Bou19b
]
a special but already quite non-trivial case of
Conjecture
0.4.3
.

Theorem 0.4.4
.

(
[
Bou19b
, Theorem 0.5.2]
)
For every fixed
d\geq 1
, we have
Ib_{j}(M_{(0,d,\chi)})=Ib_{j}(M_{(0,d,\chi^{\prime})})

as long as
\gcd(d,\chi)=\gcd(d,\chi^{\prime})
.

Using the algorithm given by Theorem
0.1.1
to compute the intersection Betti numbers
Ib_{j}(M_{(0,d,\chi)})
, we can practically test Conjecture
0.4.3

in low degrees.

Theorem 0.4.5
.

Conjecture
0.4.3
holds for
d\leq 4
.

As the intersection Betti numbers are computed by the scattering diagram

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
, one might hope for a combinatorial proof of Conjecture

0.4.3
. This seems quite difficult. In particular, we will see explicitly in the proof of Theorem
0.4.5

in §
6.4

that the
tree decompositions of the Betti numbers are different for different values of
\chi
, and only the total sum over the trees is
\chi
-independent in a seemingly miraculous way.

0.5.
Relations with previous and future works

In this section we briefly mention some related works.

0.5.1.
Bridgeland

The idea to consider objects with fixed argument of the central charge in order to make a connection between scattering diagrams
and stability conditions, in the context of quiver with potentials, is already contained in

[
Bri17
]
. Given
(Q,W)
a quiver with potential, let
Q_{0}
be its set of vertices and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}
be the lattice of dimension for representations of
Q
. We denote
\Gamma^{\vee}\coloneq\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z})
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneq\Gamma^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the triangulated category of dg-modules over the Ginzburg algebra of
(Q,W)
with finite-dimensional cohomology.
It is a triangulated category, Calabi-Yau of dimension
3
, with a natural bounded
t
-structure of heart the abelian category

\mathcal{A}
of finite-dimensional representations of the Jacobi algebra of
(Q,W)
.
We have a natural embedding

\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\operatorname{Stab}(\mathcal{C})\,,

\theta\mapsto(Z_{\theta},\mathcal{A})\,,

with the central charge
Z^{\theta}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by

Z^{\theta}=-\theta+i\delta\,,

where

\delta\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

(\gamma_{j})_{j\in Q_{0}}\mapsto\sum_{j\in Q_{0}}\gamma_{j}\,,

is the
total dimension for representations of
Q
.
The main result of
[
Bri17
]

is the construction, in terms of Donaldson-Thomas invariants of

\mathcal{C}
, of a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{(Q,W)}
on

\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, supported on the loci of points
\theta\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
such that there exists

\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\theta}_{\gamma}=-\theta(\gamma)=0
and such that there exists a
\theta
-semistable object of class
\gamma
.

Our construction of the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
on
U
is similar. The main difference is that,
in
[
Bri17
]
,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
has a natural structure of vector space,
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{(Q,W)}
is local, that is, everything non-trivial happens near
0\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
, corresponding to the fact that the abelian heart
\mathcal{A}
is fixed, whereas
U
is only a piece of an integral affine manifold,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
involves infinitely many such local scatterings, corresponding to the fact that the abelian heart is moving in the triangulated category

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Another result of
[
Bri17
]
is a correspondence between the chamber structure of

\mathfrak{D}^{(Q,W)}
and the mutations of

(Q,W)
. We will establish in a follow-up paper a similar
correspondence between the chamber structure of

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and the exceptional collections of

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
related by mutations, see §
0.5.5
for more details.

For every choice of exceptional collection
\mathcal{E}
on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
, there is a quiver with potential

(Q_{\mathcal{E}},W_{\mathcal{E}})
describing
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
, and so we can apply
[
Bri17
]
to obtain a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{(Q_{\mathcal{E}},W_{\mathcal{E}})}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. It would be interesting to obtain a precise comparison between
\mathfrak{D}^{(Q_{\mathcal{E}},W_{\mathcal{E}})}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
.

0.5.2.
Kontsevich-Soibelman

In

[
KS14
]
, Kontsevich-Soibelman introduce a general notion of wall-crossing
structure and give several, mainly conjectural, constructions.
Under some assumptions, they construct a wall-crossing structure on the base

B
of an holomorphic integrable system
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
.
When
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
is of Hitchin type, there is an associated
noncompact Calabi-Yau 3-fold
Y
and a conjectural embedding of (the universal cover of)
the complement
B_{0}
of the discriminant locus into the space of Bridgeland stability conditions of the Fukaya category

F(Y)
of
Y
. General Donaldson-Thomas theory, conjecturally applied to
F(Y)
,
naturally defines a wall-crossing structure on
B
. Kontsevich-Soibelman conjecture
that the wall-crossing structures on
B

coming from the holomorphic integrable system
\pi\colon M\rightarrow B
and
from Donaldson-Thomas theory of
F(Y)
coincide.

In
[
Bou19b
, §6]
, we explain how our main result fits into this general framework, by taking for
M

the mirror of
(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
and for
Y
the mirror of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.
In particular, combining hyperkähler rotation and mirror symmetry, we will obtain a conceptual heuristic explanation for why the main connection established in
[
Bou19b
]
between sheaf counting on

K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
and relative Gromov-Witten theory of

(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
should be true:
under hyperkähler rotation, holomorphic curves in
\mathbb{P}^{2}-E
turn into open special Lagrangians in
M
, which after suspension turn into closed special Lagrangians in
Y
, which under mirror symmetry turn into objects of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
.

Remark that
M

is not of Hitchin type and so we are slightly outside of the strict framework of

[
KS14
]
but we are in a natural extension of it. The main reason why we are able to extract non-conjectural statements from this story is that, in this specific example,
Y
has a reasonable mirror
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, and so we can work with the algebro-geometric
D^{b}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
instead of the a priori complicated looking symplectic Fukaya category
F(Y)
.

0.5.3.
Physics

Hodge numbers of intersection cohomology of
moduli spaces of Bridgeland semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
are BPS indices of the
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric 4-dimensional theory obtained by compactifying
type IIA string theory on the local Calabi-Yau 3-fold
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.

Even if our slice
U
in the space of stability conditions is defined by some polynomial
central charge, we claim that the resulting scattering diagram would stay the same for the slice given by the vector multiplet moduli of the
\mathcal{N}=2
theory, that is, by the stringy Kähler moduli space of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
where the central charge is given by the periods solutions of the mirror Picard-Fuchs equation
(see e.g.
[
BM11
, §9]
). More details on this claim are given in

[
Bou19b
, §3.3]
. In fact, the main constructions of the present paper were first tested using the physical central charge given by the periods.
We choose to write the present paper using polynomial
central charge for convenience: contact with the existing mathematical litterature is easier and change of variables given by algebraic functions are easier to study than those given by transcendental functions. Nevertheless, we remark that in order to generalize the present paper to more general geometries, polynomial central charges might not be good enough in general, and the use of the honest stringy Kähler moduli space might be ultimately necessary.

The
\mathcal{N}=2
theory obtained by compactifying type IIA string theory on

K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
has been studied early on
[
DFR05
]
(and in fact, as part of a series of physics works which motivated the definition of Bridgeland stability conditions). At the time of
[
DFR05
]
, the wall-crossing formula of Kontsevich-Soibelman and Joyce-Song was not known and only some qualitative aspects of the question of relating the BPS spectrum near the orbifold point to the BPS spectrum near the large volume point were studied.
Using the wall-crossing formula, the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

gives in some sense a complete answer to this physics question, in a form which does not seem to have appeared before in the physics literature.
At the level of terminology, we remark that the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is made of
K
-walls in the sense of
[
GMN13
]
.

The study of the BPS spectrum of
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
has been revisited
more recently using spectral networks

[
ESW17
]
and partial results have been obtained.
Our approach is orthogonal: whereas the spectral networks live on appropriate projections of the mirror curves to
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, our scattering diagram naturally lives on the base of the family parametrizing these mirror curves (see §
0.5.2
).
In physics language, the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is a mathematical version of the string junction description of the BPS spectrum in the
D3
-brane probe realization of the
\mathcal{N}=2
theory (see
[
MNS98
]
and follow-ups).

0.5.4.
Manschot

An alternative algorithm to compute Hodge numbers of intersection cohomology
of moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, at least for sheaves of positive rank, has been developed by Manschot

[
Man11
,
Man13
,
Man17
,
MM18
]
. The idea, going back to Yoshioka

[
Yos94
,
Yos95
]
in rank
2
, is to blow-up one point on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, reduce the problem to the blown-up
surface by some blow-up formula, and then solve the problem on the blown-up surface by wall-crossing in the space of polarizations of the blown-up surface.

This algorithm and the algorithm given by our Theorem

0.1.1
are similar in the sense that they both use wall-crossing in a space of stability conditions. But there is an important difference: the space of stability conditions in Manschot’s algorithm is a space of polarizations, at the cost of working with an auxiliary geometry and using a blow-up formula, whereas our algorithm only uses the geometry intrinsic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, at the cost of going deep in the interior of the space of Bridgeland stability conditions.
Another difference is that Manschot’s algorithm works rank by rank, and seems limited to sheaves of positive rank, whereas our algorithm considers all ranks at once and is able to treat sheaves supported in dimension
1
.

With his approach, Manschot is able to get explicit formulas for generating series of invariants at fixed rank and degree, at least
in low ranks. It is not clear how to do the same with our algorithm and it is some possibly interesting question to explore.

0.5.5.
Exceptional vector bundles and mutations

As we will review in
[
Bou19b
, §3]
, some specialization
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})

of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
naturally appears in the Gross-Siebert description of mirror symmetry for the pair

(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
, where
E
is a smooth cubic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}

[
CPS10
]
.
The scattering diagram

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
has been further studied following this point of view in

[
Pri20
]
. In particular, the main result of
[
Pri20
]

is the existence in the complement of the support of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
of a decomposition in triangle chambers naturally
indexed and related by combinatorial mutations
[
ACGK12
]
.

On the other hand, exactly the same combinatorics of mutations is well-known to describe exceptional collections in

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

[
DLP85
,
Dre86
,
Dre87
,
GR87
,
Rud88
]
.
As we define the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
in terms of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, and our main result (Theorem
0.1.1
) is the equality

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
, it is natural to ask if the decomposition in triangles of
[
Pri20
]
has a
natural interpretation in terms of exceptional collections from the point of view of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
.

One can show that it is indeed the case, the sides of these triangles are of the form

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(E_{1})}=0
,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(E_{2})}=0
,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(E_{3})}=0
for
(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})
an exceptional collection in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, and the combinatorial mutations of triangles match the mutations of exceptional collections.
In particular, the equality
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
gives a new and intrinsic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
explanation of the common appearance of the combinatorics of mutations in the mirror construction for
(\mathbb{P}^{2},E)
and in the structure of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. Here intrinsic means without using
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein degenerations of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, which are naturally related to both combinatorial mutations and to exceptional collections
[
Hac13
]
. To find such explanation was in fact one of our original motivations for Theorem
0.1.1
.

In order to keep the length of the present paper finite, the claims of the above paragraph will be discussed in a follow-up paper. Finally,
we remark that our proof of Theorem
0.1.1

is logically independent of the classical results of Drézet and Le Potier
[
DLP85
]
on the classification of exceptional vector bundles and on the criterion for nonemptiness of the moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves. Therefore, we might expect Theorem
0.1.1
to give a new approach to these results, and we also refer to some future work.

0.6.
Plan of the paper

In

section

1
we
introduce general notions about scattering diagrams and we define in a purely algorithmic way a scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.
In
section

2

we introduce specific coordinates on a slice of the space of Bridgeland stability conditions on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
and we use them to define a scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
in terms of intersection cohomology of Bridgeland semistable objects.
In
section

3

we prove that the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is consistent.
In
section

4

we show that the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
have in some sense the same initial data.
In
section

5

we prove
Theorem
0.1.1
,
that is, the equality
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
. In

section

6

we discuss various applications to moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves.

0.7.

[
Bou19b
]
. I thank Richard Thomas and Tom Bridgeland for discussions at a stage where some of the ideas ultimately incorporated in this paper were still in preliminary form. I thank Yu-Shen Lin for an early discussion on the scattering diagram of
[
CPS10
]
. I thank Tim Gabele for discussions on his work
[
Gab19
]
. I thank Michel van Garrel, Penka Georgieva, Rahul Pandharipande, Vivek Shende and Bernd Siebert for invitations to conferences and seminars where this work has been presented and for related discussions.

I acknowledge the support of Dr. Max Rössler, the Walter Haefner Foundation and the ETH Zürich
Foundation.

1.
The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

In
section

1.1
we review the notion of local scattering diagram.
In
section

1.2
we give a definition of scattering diagram specifically designed for our future needs. Some slightly unusual
feature is the consideration of real, not just integral, powers of the
deformation parameter
t
.
In
section

1.3
we give a general construction of initial scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
and of their consistent completions
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
. We specialize
this construction in
section

1.4
in order to define scattering diagrams
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
,
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{\pm}})
, and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{\pm}})
.
In
section

1.5
we establish a symmetry property of the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

We follow to various degrees the notation and conventions of the references

[
GS11
,
GPS10
,
Gro11
,
Bou18
]
, except that what we call local scattering diagrams are the scattering diagrams of
[
GS11
]
, and what we call scattering diagrams are the wall structures
of
[
GS11
]
. This slight change in terminology is designed in order to avoid possible confusions with the terminology usually used in the context of Bridgeland stability conditions, which will enter the story in
section

2
.

1.1.
Local scattering diagrams

In this section, we fix

M\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
a two-dimensional lattice,

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

a non-degenerate skew-symmetric integral bilinear form on

M
,

\varphi\colon M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

m\mapsto\varphi(m)\,,

an additive real-valued function on
M
,
and

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathfrak{g}_{m}\,,

a
M
-graded Lie algebra over

\mathbb{Q}
(that is, with
[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{m+m^{\prime}}
) such that

[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]=0
if

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle=0
.

Let
t
be some formal variable. We denote
\mathbb{Q}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]
the
commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra of the monoid
(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0},+)
, that is, the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear basis of monomials
t^{r}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, and with product given by

t^{r}\cdot t^{r^{\prime}}\coloneqq t^{r+r^{\prime}}\,.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]
the

\mathbb{Q}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]
-Lie algebra with underlying
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]
and with bracket defined by

[g\,t^{r},g^{\prime}\,t^{r^{\prime}}]\coloneq[g,g^{\prime}]\,t^{r+r^{\prime}}\,,

for every
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
and
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, we denote

\mathfrak{m}_{k}
the ideal of

\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]

generated by elements of the form
g\,t^{r}
with
g\in\mathfrak{g}
and
r>k
.

Definition 1.1.1
.

For every nonzero
m\in M
, a
local naked ray of class
m
is a subset of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
of the form either

\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m
or
-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m
.

Definition 1.1.2
.

For every nonzero
m\in M
with

\varphi(m)\geqslant 0
, a
local ray
\mathfrak{d}
of class
m

for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
is a pair

(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
, where:

•

|\mathfrak{d}|
is a local naked ray of class
m
.

•

H_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]
is of the form

H_{\mathfrak{d}}=\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}\,t^{\varphi(m)}\,,

for some nonzero
\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}
.

The local ray
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H)
of class
m
is
outgoing
if

|\mathfrak{d}|=-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m
, and
ingoing
if

|\mathfrak{d}|=\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m
.
The local naked ray
|\mathfrak{d}|
is called the support of the local ray
\mathfrak{d}
.

It follows from Definition

1.1.2
that the class of a local ray is uniquely determined by the local ray.

Definition 1.1.3
.

We denote
m_{\mathfrak{d}}\in M
the class of a local ray
\mathfrak{d}
.

Definition 1.1.4
.

A
local scattering diagram
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
is a collection
\mathfrak{D}
of local rays
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
, such that:

(1)

For every nonzero
m\in M
, there is at most one ingoing local ray of class
m
in
\mathfrak{D}
, and at most one outgoing ray of class
m
in
\mathfrak{D}
.

(2)

For every ray
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
\mathfrak{D}
, we have

\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0
.

(3)

For every
k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, there are only finitely many rays

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
\mathfrak{D}
with

\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k\,,

that is, with

H_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{k}\,.

Definition 1.1.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a local scattering diagram
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
. For every ray

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
of
\mathfrak{D}
and for every positive integer
k
, we denote
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d},k}

the automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}

given by:

\Phi_{\mathfrak{d},k}\coloneqq\exp([H_{\mathfrak{d}},-])\,.

The definition of
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d},k}
makes sense as

H_{\mathfrak{d}}=\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}\,t^{\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})}
with
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0
by condition (2) of Definition
1.1.4
.

Definition 1.1.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a local scattering diagram
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
. We fix a positive integer
k
, and some smooth path

\alpha\colon[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}-\{0\}\,

t\mapsto\alpha(t)\,,

with transverse intersection with respect to all the rays
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D}

with
H_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{k}
. Let
\mathfrak{d}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{d}_{N}
be the successive rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
\mathfrak{D}
with
H_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{k}
intersected by the path

\alpha
at times
t_{1}\leqslant\dots\leqslant t_{N}
.
Then,
the
order
k
automorphism associated to
\alpha
, denoted
\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, is the automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}

defined by

\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{N},k}^{\epsilon_{N}}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{1},k}^{\epsilon_{1}}\,,

where, for every
j=1,\dots,N
,

\epsilon_{j}\coloneq\mathrm{sign}(\det(\alpha^{\prime}(t_{j}),m_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}))\in\{\pm 1\}\,.

Remarks:

•

The ordering of the rays

\mathfrak{d}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{d}_{N}
by times of intersection with the path is slightly ambiguous as different rays can have the
same support. By the assumption
[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]=0
if

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle=0
, the automorphisms associated to rays with the same support commute. It follows that

\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}
is well-defined despite this ambiguity.

•

The definition of
\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}
makes sense as there are only finitely many rays
\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathfrak{D}

with
H_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{k}
by condition
(2) of Definition
1.1.4
.

Definition 1.1.7
.

Let
k
be a nonnegative integer.
A local scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
is
consistent at order
k

if, for every

\alpha\colon[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}-\{0\}\,,

t\mapsto\alpha(t)\,,

with
\alpha(0)=\alpha(1)
,
smooth loop in
M_{\mathbb{R}}-\{0\}
, with transverse intersection with respect to all the rays
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
\mathfrak{D}

with
H_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{k}
, the order
k
automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}
associated to
\alpha
is the identity:

\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}=\operatorname{id}\,\,.

In order to check order

k
consistency of a local scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, it is enough to show that

\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{\mathfrak{D}}=\operatorname{id}\,
for
\alpha
a simple smooth loop in
M_{\mathbb{R}}-\{0\}
encircling
0
and with transverse intersection with respect to all rays of
\mathfrak{D}
.

Definition 1.1.8
.

A local scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
is
consistent
if it is consistent at order
k

for every nonnegative integer
k
.

The following Proposition
1.1.9

states that, under some condition on the

t
-adic valuation of the initial rays, any local scattering diagram can be completed in a consistent one.
The shape of this result goes back to Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS06
, Theorem 6]
. Many variants exist in the literature, see for example

[
GPS10
, Theorem 1.4]
.
We write down below the
‘usual’ proof in order to check that it goes through our slightly unusual conventions, such that the fact that
\varphi
is
\mathbb{R}
-valued (and not
\mathbb{Z}
-valued).

Proposition 1.1.9
.

We fix
c
a real number such that
c\geqslant 1
.
Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a local scattering diagram for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
such that, for every ray
\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathfrak{D}
, we have
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant c
. Then, there exists a unique sequence

(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of local scattering diagram
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
,
such that:

•

S_{0}(\mathfrak{D})
is the set of ingoing rays of
\mathfrak{D}
.

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
is obtained by adding to
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
rays

\mathfrak{d}
such that
k<\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k+1
and
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant c
.

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
is consistent at order
k
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we call

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
the order
k

consistent completion
of
\mathfrak{D}
. We denote
S(\mathfrak{D})
the limiting consistent local scattering diagram for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})

obtained for
k\rightarrow+\infty
, and we call it the
consistent completion
of
\mathfrak{D}
.

Proof.

We prove the existence and uniqueness of

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

For
k=0
, we take for
S_{0}(\mathfrak{D})
the union of ingoing rays of
\mathfrak{D}
. By condition (2) of Definition
1.1.4
, for every ray
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
of
\mathfrak{D}
, we have
H_{\mathfrak{d}}=0\mod\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, so
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d},0}=\operatorname{id}\,
. In particular,
S_{0}(\mathfrak{D})
is consistent at order zero. The uniqueness of
S_{0}(\mathfrak{D})
is clear.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
has been constructed and proved to be unique. We wish to construct and prove the uniqueness of
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
. Let

\alpha\colon[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}-\{0\}\,,

be a simple anticlockwise loop around
0\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
. By the induction hypothesis,

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
is consistent at order
k
and so
\Phi_{\alpha,k}^{S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})}=\operatorname{id}\,
. By condition (3) of Definition
1.1.4
, for every ray

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
, we have
H_{\mathfrak{d}}
of the form
\overline{\Omega}\,t^{\varphi(m)}
for some
\overline{\Omega}\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}
. Using the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff-formula, and the facts that
\mathfrak{g}
is
M
-graded and that
\varphi
is additive, we can uniquely write

\Phi_{\alpha,k+1}^{S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})}=\exp([H^{(k+1)},-])\,

where
H^{(k+1)}\in\mathfrak{g}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k+1}
is of the form

H^{(k+1)}=\sum_{m\in M}\overline{\Omega}^{(k+1)}_{m}t^{\varphi(m)}\,

with
\overline{\Omega}^{(k+1)}_{m}\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}
and
\overline{\Omega}^{(k+1)}_{m}
possibly nonzero only if

k<\varphi(m)\leqslant k+1\,.

It follows from
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant c\geqslant 1
for every ray
\mathfrak{d}
of

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
, from condition (3) of Definition
1.1.4
satisfied by
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
, and from the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula that the set
M^{(k+1)}
of
m\in M
such that

\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}\neq 0
is finite, and that, for every such
m\in M^{(k+1)}
, we have
\varphi(m)\geqslant c
.

We define
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
as being the set of the following rays:

•

The ingoing rays of
\mathfrak{D}
, which are also the ingoing rays of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
by induction hypothesis.

•

The outgoing rays of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
.

•

For every
m\in M^{(k+1)}
,
the outgoing ray

(-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m,-\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}\,t^{\varphi(m)})\,.

By construction,
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
is consistent at order
k+1
. Indeed, by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, it is
consistent up to terms whose
t
-adic valuation is at least the one of the commutators of the form

[\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}\,t^{\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})},\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}\,t^{\varphi(m)})]=[\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}},\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}]t^{\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}}+m)}\,,

where
\mathfrak{d}
is either an incoming ray or an outgoing ray of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
, so with

\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant c
, and where

m\in M^{(k+1)}
so
\varphi(m)>k
,
or of the form

[\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}\,t^{\varphi(m)},\overline{\Omega}_{m^{\prime}}^{(k+1)}\,t^{\varphi(m^{\prime})})]=[\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)},\overline{\Omega}_{m^{\prime}}^{(k+1)}]t^{\varphi(m+m^{\prime})}\,,

where
m,m^{\prime}\in M^{(k+1)}
, and so with
\varphi(m+m^{\prime})\geqslant 2c\geqslant 2
and
\varphi(m+m^{\prime})>2k
.
In the first case, the
t
-adic valuation of the commutator is strictly greater than
c+k\geqslant k+1
.
In the second case,
if
k=0
, we have
\varphi(m+m^{\prime})\geqslant 2>1=k+1
, and if
k\geqslant 1
, we have
\varphi(m+m^{\prime})>2k\geqslant k+1
, and so the
t
-adic valuation of the commutator is also strictly greater than
k+1
in any case.

It remains to show the uniqueness of

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
. The outgoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
of

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
with
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k
are uniquely fixed by the uniqueness of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
given by the induction hypothesis. The same study of

t
-adic valuations of commutators as above shows that the order
k+1
automorphisms associated with
the outgoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})

with
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>k
commute with the order
k+1
automorphisms associated with rays of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D})
, and so outgoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D})
are uniquely determined by the condition of consistency at order
k+1
in terms of
\overline{\Omega}_{m}^{(k+1)}
, as above.
∎

Remarks:

•

The proof of Proposition
1.1.9
is the
‘usual’ proof of existence of a consistent completion of a scattering diagram, as in
[
KS06
, Theorem 6]

or

[
GPS10
, Theorem 1.4]
.
The only possibly subtle point is that
\varphi
is

\mathbb{R}
-valued, whereas in the ‘usual’ situation,
\varphi
is
\mathbb{Z}
-valued. One might worry that this could allow a phenomenon of accumulation of values of
\varphi
at some finite value, preventing the induction step to go from
k
to
k+1
.
The above proof shows that it does not happen under the additional assumption that
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
for every ray

\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathfrak{D}
.

•

It is clear from the proof of Proposition

1.1.9
that the construction of
S(\mathfrak{D})
from
\mathfrak{D}
is completely algorithmic, involving finitely many computations for any fixed order
k
.

The following Lemma motivates the scattering terminology: if ingoing rays come in a given cone of directions, then the outgoing rays of the consistent completion are ‘emitted forward’.

Lemma 1.1.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a local scattering diagram for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi,\mathfrak{g})
consisting entirely of ingoing rays contained in a strictly convex cone of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
delimited by two ingoing rays

\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}
. Then, all the outgoing rays of
S(\mathfrak{D})
are contained in the strictly convex cone
-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}}
, delimited by two outgoing rays

\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{out}}
, respectively of class

m_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}
and
m_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}}
, and with
H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}

and
H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{out}}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}}
.

Proof.

We use the perturbation trick of

[
GPS10
, §1.4]
in order to reduce the question to the case of an ‘elementary’ local scattering diagram, as

[
GPS10
, Lemma 1.9]

(see

[
Man15
, Lemma 2.9]

for the case of a general Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
), for which the result is clear. Indeed, a consistent elementary local scattering diagram has two ingoing rays of class
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, and three outgoing rays of class
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
and
m_{1}+m_{2}
. Furthermore, the outgoing rays of class
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are continuations of the two ingoing rays.
∎

1.2.
Scattering diagrams

In this section, we fix

M\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
a two-dimensional lattice,

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

a non-degenerate skew-symmetric integral bilinear form on

M
,
and

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathfrak{g}_{m}\,,

a
M
-graded Lie algebra over

\mathbb{Q}
(that is, with
[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{m+m^{\prime}}
) such that

[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]=0
if

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle=0
.

We also fix
U
an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, of closure
\bar{U}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For every
\sigma\in U
, we think about
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes\mathbb{R}
as being the tangent space to
U
at
\sigma
.

Definition 1.2.1
.

For every
m\in M
, a
naked ray of class

m
in
U
is a subset
|\mathfrak{d}|
of
\bar{U}

of the form

|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m\,,

for some

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, or of the form

|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m\,,

for some

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and some
T_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

In both cases, we call
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})
the initial point of
|\mathfrak{d}|
. If

|\mathfrak{d}|
is bounded, that is, if

|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m
, we call

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-T_{\mathfrak{d}}m
the endpoint of
|\mathfrak{d}|
.

Definition 1.2.2
.

For every
m\in M
, a
ray
\mathfrak{d}
of class

m
in
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
is a pair

(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
, where

•

|\mathfrak{d}|
is a naked ray of class
m
in
U
,

•

H_{\mathfrak{d}}
is a nonzero element of

\mathfrak{g}_{m}
.

It follows from Definition

1.2.2
that the class of a ray is uniquely determined by the ray.

Definition 1.2.3
.

We denote

m_{\mathfrak{d}}\in M
the class of a ray
\mathfrak{d}
.

Definition 1.2.4
.

For every
\sigma=(x,y)\in\bar{U}
, we denote
4
4

4

The function that we denote

\varphi_{\sigma}
should be denoted

-\varphi_{\sigma}
using the notation of
[
GS11
,
Gro11
]
. Given our conventions in
Definition
1.2.7
, the descriptions are equivalent. The notation of
[
GS11
,
Gro11
]
is the most natural from the point of view of the mirror construction, but, for the purposes of the present paper, we prefer to incorporate a minus sign once and for all in the definition of
\varphi_{\sigma}
in order to have less signs in the later formulas.

\varphi_{\sigma}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

(a,b)\mapsto 2(-ax-b)\,.

For every
\sigma\in\bar{U}
, the function

\varphi_{\sigma}
restricted to
M
is an additive real-valued function on
M
.

Lemma 1.2.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
be a ray in
U
.
Then the function given by

\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

t\mapsto\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})

if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
, or by

[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

t\mapsto\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})

if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m_{\mathfrak{d}}
,
is increasing. More precisely, writing

m_{\mathfrak{d}}=(a,b)
, this function is strictly increasing if
a\neq 0
and constant if
a=0
.

Moreover, if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m_{\mathfrak{d}}
, writing
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})=(x_{i},y_{i})
and

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-T_{\mathfrak{d}}m_{\mathfrak{d}}=(x_{f},y_{f})
, we have

\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-T_{\mathfrak{d}}m_{\mathfrak{d}}}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})-\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 2|x_{f}-x_{i}|\,.

Proof.

We write
m_{\mathfrak{d}}=(a,b)
. If
a\geqslant 0
, then the
x
-coordinate
x(t)
of
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}

is an decreasing function of
t
, and so

\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})=2(-ax(t)-b)
is an increasing function of
t
, strictly increasing if
a\neq 0
and constant if
a=0
.

Similarly, if
a<0
, then the
x
-coordinate
x(t)
of
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}

is an increasing function of
t
, and so

\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})=2(-ax(t)-b)
is a strictly increasing function of
t
.
∎

Definition 1.2.6
.

A collection
\mathfrak{D}
of rays

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{d})
is
normalized
if:

(1)

For every
\sigma\in U
and for every nonzero
m\in M
, there is at most
one ray
\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathfrak{D}
of class
m
such that

\sigma
belongs to the interior of

|\mathfrak{d}|
.

(2)

There do no exist rays
\mathfrak{d}_{1}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}})
in
\mathfrak{D}
such that the endpoint of
|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|
coincides with the initial point of
|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|
, and such that

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}}
.

Given a collection

\mathfrak{D}
of rays in
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{d})
, there is a canonical way to produce a normalized collection of rays that we call the

normalization
of
\mathfrak{D}
.
The normalization of
\mathfrak{D}
is obtained by repeated use of the following operations:

(1)

If
\mathfrak{d}_{1}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}})
are two rays of the same class
m
with
|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|
of nonempty interior, replace

\mathfrak{d}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
by the rays

(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|-|\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}})
,

(|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|-|\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}})
,

(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}+H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}})
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
are two rays
of
\mathfrak{D}
such that the endpoint of

|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|
coincides with the initial point of
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
and such that

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}}
, replace

\mathfrak{d}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
by the ray

(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|\cup|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}})
.

Definition 1.2.7
.

A
scattering diagram
on
U

for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
is a collection
\mathfrak{D}
of rays

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
, such that:

(1)

The collection
\mathfrak{D}
is normalized.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
is a ray in
\mathfrak{D}
, then
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0
for every
\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}|\cap U
.

(3)

For every compact set
K
in
U
and for every
k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, the set of rays

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
\mathfrak{D}
such that there exists
\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}|\cap K

such that
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k
is finite.

Definition 1.2.8
.

The
singular locus

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
of a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is the set of the
initial points of the rays and of the non-trivial intersection points of the rays,
that is,

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})\coloneq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}\{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\}\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{2}\in\mathfrak{D}\\
\dim|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|=0\end{subarray}}(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{2}|)\,.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
,
and let
\sigma\in U
. We explain how to define a local scattering
diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi_{\sigma},\mathfrak{g})
in the sense of
section

1.1
.
The local scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
is a local picture of
\mathfrak{D}
around the point
\sigma
,
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes\mathbb{R}
being identified with the tangent space to
U
at
\sigma
.
More precisely, local rays of
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
are constructed from rays of
\mathfrak{D}
as follows.

Let
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
be a ray of class
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
of
\mathfrak{D}
such that
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})=\sigma
. Then we define

|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}|\coloneq-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
and we view
\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}\coloneq(|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}}t^{\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})})
as a (outgoing) local ray of
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
be a ray of class
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
of
\mathfrak{D}
such that
\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}|
and

\sigma\neq\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})
. Then we define
|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,out}|\coloneq-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
and we view
\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,out}\coloneq(|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,out}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}}t^{\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})})
as a (outgoing) local ray of
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
. We also define

|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,in}|\coloneq\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
and we view

\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,in}\coloneq(|\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma,in}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}}t^{\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})})
as a (ingoing) local ray of
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 1.2.9
.

The collection of local rays
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
is a local scattering diagram for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,

\varphi_{\sigma},\mathfrak{g})

in the sense of Definition
1.1.4
.

Proof.

Conditions (1)-(2)-(3) of Definition
1.1.4
for
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
follow respectively from conditions (1)-(2)-(3) of Definition

1.2.7
for
\mathfrak{D}
.
∎

Definition 1.2.10
.

Let
k
be a nonnegative integer.
A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
on
U

for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})

is

consistent at order
k
if, for every
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
, the local scattering
diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi_{\sigma},\mathfrak{g})
is consistent at order
k
in the sense of Definition
1.1.7
.

Definition 1.2.11
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

on
U

for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})

is

consistent
if it is consistent at order
k
for every nonnegative integer
k
, or equivalently if for every
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
, the local scattering
diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi_{\sigma},\mathfrak{g})
is consistent in the sense of Definition
1.1.8
.

1.3.
The scattering diagrams
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})

In this section we construct specific examples of scattering diagrams in the sense of
section

1.2
.

Recall that we fix
M\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
a
two-dimensional lattice.
We take

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

given by

\langle(a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle=3(a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime})\,.

The precise form of
\langle-,-\rangle
plays no role
in the remaining part of
section

1
, but will become
crucial for the wall-crossing argument in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
: the choice of

\langle-,-\rangle
is dictated by the skew-symmetrized Euler form of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
,
see Lemma
2.1.3
.

We consider

U
the open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

U=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}\,.

We have

\bar{U}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,y\geqslant-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}\,,

and so the boundary
\partial U\coloneq\bar{U}-U
is the parabola of equation
y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
.

Lemma 1.3.1
.

Let
\sigma\in U
and

m\in M
such that

\varphi_{\sigma}(m)>0
. Then

\sigma-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m\subset U\,.

Proof.

We write
\sigma=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and

m=(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. For every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, we have

\sigma-tm=(x(t),y(t))
and

(x-ta)^{2}+2(y-tb)=x^{2}+2y+2t(-ax-b)+t^{2}a^{2}\geqslant x^{2}+2y+2t(-ax-b)\,.

As
\sigma\in U
, we have
x^{2}+2y>0
.
On the other hand, we have
\varphi_{\sigma}(m)=2(-ax-b)\geqslant 0
. It follows that
(x-ta)^{2}+2(y-tb)>0
, that is,

\sigma-tm\in U
.
∎

Lemma 1.3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}
be a ray in
U
such that

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0
for every

\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}|\cap U
.
Then, writing

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-tm_{\mathfrak{d}}=(x(t),y(t))
for every

t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, the function given by

\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

t\mapsto x(t)^{2}+2y(t)

if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant m_{\mathfrak{d}}}
, or by

[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

t\mapsto x(t)^{2}+2y(t)

if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m_{\mathfrak{d}}
, is strictly increasing.

Proof.

Writing
m_{\mathfrak{d}}=(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we have, for every

\sigma=(x(t),y(t))\in|\mathfrak{d}|\cap U
,

\frac{d}{dt}(x(t)^{2}+2y(t))=2(-ax(t)-b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0\,.

As
|\mathfrak{d}|\cap\bar{U}
is at most a
point (indeed,
|\mathfrak{d}|\subset\bar{U}

and
\partial{U}
is a parabola), and

t\mapsto x(t)^{2}+2y(t)
is continuous, we conclude that

t\mapsto x(t)^{2}+2y(t)
is strictly increasing.
∎

Definition 1.3.3
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote

s_{n}\coloneq(n,-\frac{n^{2}}{2})\in\bar{U}-U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\,

m_{n}^{-}\coloneq(1,-n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\,

m_{n}^{+}\coloneq(-1,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\,.

Definition 1.3.4
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define naked rays

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\coloneq s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{+}\,,

and

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|\coloneq s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{-}\,.

We have
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap U=s_{n}-(0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{+}
and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|\cap U\coloneq s_{n}-(0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{-}
,
that is,
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
are contained in
U
, except for their initial point
s_{n}
which is on the boundary of
U
. In particular,

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
are naked rays in
U
in the sense of Definition

1.2.1
. This is related to the fact that, for every

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the line
\mathbb{R}m_{n}^{-}=\mathbb{R}m_{n}^{+}

is the tangent at the point
s_{n}
to the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, that is, to the boundary of
U
.

Lemma 1.3.5
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|=s_{n}-\frac{1}{2}m_{n}^{+}=s_{n+1}-\frac{1}{2}m_{n+1}^{-}=(n+\frac{1}{2},-\frac{n^{2}}{2}-\frac{n}{2})\,.

Proof.

Elementary computation.
∎

Lemma 1.3.6
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\varphi_{|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|}(m_{n}^{+})=\varphi_{|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|}(m_{n+1}^{-})=1\,.

Proof.

Using Definition
1.2.4
and Lemma
1.3.5
, we have

\varphi_{|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|}(m_{n}^{+})=2(n+\frac{1}{2}-n)=1\,,

and

\varphi_{|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|}(m_{n+1}^{-})=2(-n-\frac{1}{2}+n+1)=1\,.

∎

Figure 3.
The parabola
y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}

and the tangent lines

\mathbb{R}m_{n}^{-}=\mathbb{R}m_{n}^{+}
at the points
s_{n}
.

Let

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathfrak{g}_{m}\,,

a
M
-graded Lie algebra over

\mathbb{Q}
(that is with
[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{m+m^{\prime}}
) such that

[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]=0
if

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,
we fix nonzero elements

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer

\ell\geqslant 1
,
(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|,H_{n,\ell}^{+})

and
(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|,H_{n,\ell}^{-})

are rays in
U
in the sense of Definition

1.2.2
.

Lemma 1.3.7
.

The set
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of rays

\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|,H_{n,\ell}^{+})
,
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq(|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|,H_{n,\ell}^{-})
, for all

n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, is a scattering diagram on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
in the sense of Definition
1.2.7
.

Proof.

As the rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
