have distinct classes (
\ell m_{n}^{+}=(-\ell,\ell n)
for

\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{+}
and

\ell m_{n}^{-}=(\ell,-\ell n)
for

\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{-}
),
and as there do not exist rays
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
with the endpoint of

\mathfrak{d}_{1}
coinciding with the initial point of
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfies condition (1)
of Definition
1.2.7
.

If
\sigma=(x,y)\in|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap U
, then
x>n
, so, for every
\ell\geqslant 1
,
\varphi_{\sigma}(\ell m_{n}^{+})=\ell(x-n)>0
. Similarly, if
\sigma=(x,y)\in|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|\cap U
, then
x<n
, so, for every

\ell\geqslant 1
,

\varphi_{\sigma}(\ell m_{n}^{-})=\ell(-x+n)>0
. It follows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfies condition (2) of
Definition
1.2.7
.

Let
K
be a compact in
U
and
k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
. As
K
is compact in
U
, there exists
C>0
such that
\sigma=(x,y)\in K
implies
|x|<C
, and there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\sigma=(x,y)\in K
implies
|x-n|>\epsilon
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, if we have

\sigma\in K\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
such that

\varphi_{\sigma}(\ell m_{n}^{+})\leqslant k
, then

\ell(x-n)\leqslant k
,
|x|<C
,
|x-n|>\epsilon
, so

|n|\leqslant k+C
and
\ell\leqslant k/\epsilon
: in particular, there are finitely many such
n
and
\ell
.

Similarly, if we have

\sigma\in K\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
such that

\varphi_{\sigma}(\ell m_{n}^{-})\leqslant k
, then

\ell(n-x)\leqslant k
,
|x|<C
,
|x-n|>\epsilon
, so

|n|\leqslant k+C
and
\ell\leqslant k/\epsilon
. There are again only finitely many such
n
and
\ell
. This shows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfies condition (3)
of Definition
1.2.7
.
∎

Proposition 1.3.8
.

There exists a unique sequence

(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of scattering
diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
such that:

•

S_{0}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is obtained by adding to

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
rays
\mathfrak{d}
such that

|\mathfrak{d}|\subset U
,
k<\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k+1
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
.

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is consistent at order
k
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we call
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
the order
k

consistent completion
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
. We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
the limiting consistent scattering diagram on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle)
and we call it the
consistent completion
of

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

Proof.

We prove the existence and uniqueness of

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
by induction on

k\in\mathbb{N}
.

For
k=0
, the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
is trivially consistent at order
0
, and we set
S_{0}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})\coloneq\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
According to
Lemma
1.3.5
and Lemma

1.3.6
,
the only
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
are the points
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cap|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|
, and at such point
\sigma
, the
function

\varphi_{\sigma}
evaluated on the classes of the ingoing rays of the corresponding local scattering diagram is equal to
1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
has been constructed and
proved to be unique. We wish to construct
and prove the uniqueness of
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
.

For every
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}))
, let
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
be the corresponding local scattering diagram for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\varphi_{\sigma},\mathfrak{g})_{\sigma}
given by Lemma
1.2.9
. By induction hypothesis, we know that
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
for every ray
\mathfrak{d}
of
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
. Using Proposition
1.1.9
, it follows that the local scattering diagram
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}

can be canonically completed in an
order
k+1
consistent local scattering diagram

S_{k+1}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma})
, by adding outgoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
with
k<\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k+1
and
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
.

For every
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
and for every

\mathfrak{d}=(-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}},\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}t^{\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})})
outgoing ray of
S_{k+1}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma})
added to
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
, we define a ray
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
in
U
by

\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneq(\sigma-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}},\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}})\,.

We have by construction

k<\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\tilde{\mathfrak{d}})}(m_{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}})\leqslant k+1
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\tilde{\mathfrak{d}})}(m_{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}})\geqslant 1
.
Remark that according to Lemma

1.3.1
, the condition
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1>0
implies that

\sigma-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}\subset U
, and so

\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
is indeed a ray in
U
.

We denote
\tilde{S}_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
the union of the rays of

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
and of all the rays
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
constructed above.
We define
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
as being the normalization of

\tilde{S}_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})

(see Remark following Definition

1.2.6
), so that

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
satisfies condition (1) of Definition
1.2.7

By construction,
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
satisfies condition (2) of Definition

1.2.7
. Therefore, in order to show that
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is a scattering diagram on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g})
,
it remains to check condition (3) of Definition

1.2.7
. It follows from the second part of Lemma

1.2.5
that for every compact
subset
K
of
U
, there exists
a positive integer
N
such that every ray

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
in

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})

comes by successive local scatterings from the initial rays
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}
with

|n|\leqslant N
. In particular, there are only finitely many such rays, and so condition (3) of Definition
1.2.7
is satisfied.

We claim that the scattering diagram
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is consistent at order
k+1
.
Indeed, let
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
and let
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
be the corresponding local scattering diagram.
If
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}))
, then
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
is obtained from
S_{k+1}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma})

by adding rays
\mathfrak{d}
with
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>k
and

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
, coming in ingoing-outgoing pairs (local picture of a ray passing through
\sigma
: see Definition of

\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
in
section

1.2
).
By construction,
S_{k+1}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma})

is consistent at order
k+1
. On the other hand, the automorphisms attached to the added rays commute with the automorphisms attached to the rays of
S_{k+1}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma})

and with each other up to terms of
t
-adic valuation strictly greater than
k+1
,
so the automorphisms attached to ingoing-outgoing pairs cancel each other and so

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
is consistent at order
k+1
.

If
\sigma\notin U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}))
, then all the rays

\mathfrak{d}
of
S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
are newly added rays, with
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>k

and
\varphi(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\geqslant 1
, coming in ingoing-outgoing pairs (local picture of a ray passing through
\sigma
: see Definition of

\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}
in
section

1.2
). The automorphisms attached to the added rays commute with each other up to terms of

t
-adic valuation strictly greater than
\max(2k,2)\geqslant k+1
, so the automorphisms attached to ingoing-outgoing pairs cancel each other and so

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})_{\sigma}
is consistent at order
k+1
.

It remains to prove the uniqueness of

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
.
Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
satisfying the same properties that

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
stated in Proposition

1.3.8
.
Let
\mathfrak{d}
be a ray of
\mathfrak{D}
which is not in
S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
. As
\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})}\leqslant k+1
,
\mathfrak{d}
cannot be produced by a local scattering containing as incoming ray a ray of
\mathfrak{D}
added to

S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
, and so
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}))
.
It follows by consistency of

\mathfrak{D}
and uniqueness of the local consistent completion given by Proposition

1.1.9
that

\mathfrak{d}
is one of the rays of

S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
.
∎

The proof of Proposition

1.3.8

is a variant of a rather simple case of the construction of
wall structures in the sense of Gross-Siebert (e.g. see

[
Gro11
, §6.3.3]
, or
[
GS11
]
for a much more general context). The main difference is that we do not work with
polyhedral decompositions and
\mathbb{Z}
-valued
piecewise linear functions, but with
\mathbb{R}
-valued linear functions
\varphi_{\sigma}
.

It is clear from the proof of Proposition

1.3.8
that the construction of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
from

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
is completely algorithmic:
it is an iterative application of the construction of local consistent completions,
Proposition

1.1.9
, which is itself completely algorithmic.

1.4.
The scattering diagrams
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
,
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{\pm}})
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{\pm}})

For every
\mathfrak{g}

a
M
-graded Lie algebra over

\mathbb{Q}
such that

[\mathfrak{g}_{m},\mathfrak{g}_{m^{\prime}}]=0
if

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle=0
,
and for every choice of nonzero elements

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}

and
H_{n,\ell}^{-}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\ell\geqslant 1
, we have defined
in
section

1.3
a scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
and constructed its consistent completion
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
.
Below, we apply this construction in several more specific cases.

Definition 1.4.1
.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
the
\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}((uv)^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}})z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,.

Definition 1.4.2
.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}
the
\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}(q^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}})z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,.

Definition 1.4.3
.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}}
the
\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq(q^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}})z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,.

Definition 1.4.4
.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}
the
\mathbb{Q}
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,.

Definition 1.4.5
.

We denote
\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}
the
\mathbb{Q}
-Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}\coloneq\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}z^{m}

with Lie bracket given by

[z^{m},z^{m^{\prime}}]\coloneq\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle z^{m+m^{\prime}}\,.

Remarks:

•

\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}
is the specialization
u=v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}

of
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
.

•

\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}
is the semiclassical limit
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1
of
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}
.

•

\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}
is the semiclassical
limit
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1

of
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}}
.

Definition 1.4.6
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
section

1.3

obtained for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
, and, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
the consistent completion of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.

Definition 1.4.7
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}}
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
section

1.3

obtained for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{q}
, and, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}})_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}})_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})
the consistent completion of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}}
given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.

Definition 1.4.8
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}}
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
section

1.3

obtained for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{q}
, and, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}}{\ell}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}})_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}}{\ell}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}})_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}})
the consistent completion of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}}
given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.

Definition 1.4.9
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
section

1.3

obtained for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}
, and, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq-\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})
the consistent completion of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}
given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.

Definition 1.4.10
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}
the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
section

1.3

obtained for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}
, and, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

H_{n,\ell}^{+}\coloneq\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}}{\ell^{2}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})_{\ell m_{n}^{+}}\,,

and

H_{n,\ell}^{-}\coloneq\frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}}{\ell^{2}}z^{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})_{\ell m_{n}^{-}}\,.

We denote
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
the consistent completion of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}
given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.

Lemma 1.4.11
.

•

Under the change of variables

q^{\frac{1}{2}}=(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have the equality of scattering diagrams

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

•

The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})
is the semiclassical limit
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1
of the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})
.

•

The scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
is the semiclassical
limit
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1

of the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}})
.

Proof.

The result is true at the level of the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}
from
their explicit description.
The result follows by uniqueness of the consistent completion given by
Proposition
1.3.8
.
∎

Corollary 1.4.12
.

For every ray
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
of

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
, we have

H_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})\,,

where we view
\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})

inside

\mathbb{Q}(u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
via the change of variables

q^{\frac{1}{2}}=(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

It is an immediate consequence of the first point of Lemma
1.4.11
.
∎

1.5.
Action of
\psi(1)
on scattering diagrams

In this section we establish a symmetry

\psi(1)
of the scattering diagram

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
defined in

section

1.4
.

Definition 1.5.1
.

We denote
\psi(1)
the affine transformation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by

\psi(1)\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}

(x,y)\mapsto\left(x+1,y-x-\frac{1}{2}\right)\,,

and
\mathrm{d\psi}[1]
its linear part,
given by

\mathrm{d\psi}(1)\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}

(a,b)\mapsto(a,b-a)\,.

Lemma 1.5.2
.

For every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, writing

\psi(1)((x,y))=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, we have

(x^{\prime})^{2}+2y^{\prime}=x^{2}+2y\,.

Proof.

We have

(x^{\prime})^{2}+2y^{\prime}=(x+1)^{2}+2(y-x-\frac{1}{2})=x^{2}+2y\,.

∎

Lemma 1.5.3
.

The affine transformation
\psi(1)
of

\mathbb{R}^{2}
preserves
U
.

Moreover the restriction of
\psi(1)
to
U
is a
bijection from
U
to
U
.

Proof.

Recall that
(x,y)\in U
if and only if

x^{2}+2y>0
. Therefore, the fact that
\psi(1)
preserves
U
follows directly from
Lemma
1.5.2
.

In order to show that

\psi(1)|_{U}\colon U\rightarrow U
is a bijection, we remark that

\psi(1)\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bijection, of inverse given by

\psi(1)^{-1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}

(x,y)\mapsto(x-1,y+x-\frac{1}{2})\,.

The fact that
\psi(1)^{-1}
preserves
U

follows from Lemma

1.5.2
.
∎

Definition 1.5.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
be a ray of class

m_{\mathfrak{d}}
in
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.
Writing
H_{\mathfrak{d}}=\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}z^{m_{\mathfrak{d}}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})_{m_{\mathfrak{d}}}
, we define

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{d})\coloneq(\psi(1)(|\mathfrak{d}|),\psi(1)(H_{\mathfrak{d}}))
,
where

\psi(1)(|\mathfrak{d}|)
is the image of
|\mathfrak{d}|

by
\psi(1)
, and where

\psi(1)(H_{\mathfrak{d}})\coloneq\overline{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{d}}z^{\mathrm{d\psi}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})}
.

The notation
\psi(1)
will be justified in
Lemma
2.6.1
, where it will be shown that, after identification of
U

with a space of stability conditions on

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, the action of
\psi(1)
on
U
coincides with the action of the autoequivalence
-\otimes\mathcal{O}(1)

of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Lemma 1.5.5
.

If
\mathfrak{d}
is a ray of class
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
in

U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, then
\psi(1)(\mathfrak{d})
is a ray of class

\mathrm{d\psi}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})
in
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.

Proof.

The only not completely obvious point is that
\psi(1)(|\mathfrak{d}|)
is contained in

\bar{U}
, but this follows from Lemma

1.5.3
.
∎

Definition 1.5.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
. We denote

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D})
the collection of rays

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{d})
, for all
\mathfrak{d}
ray of

\mathfrak{D}
.

Lemma 1.5.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.
Then
\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D})
is a scattering diagram on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.

Proof.

We have to check conditions (1)-(2)-(3)
of Definition
1.2.7
.
Condition (1) is clear as
\psi(1)
restricted to
U
is a bijection from
U
to
U
according to Lemma
1.5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}
be a ray of
\mathfrak{D}
and let

\sigma=(x,y)\in|\mathfrak{d}|\cap U
. Writing

m_{\mathfrak{d}}=(a,b)
, we have

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})=2(-ax-b)>0
by condition (2) of Definition

1.2.7
satisfied by
\mathfrak{D}
.
So we have

\varphi_{\psi(1)(\sigma)}(\mathrm{d\psi}(1)(m_{\mathfrak{d}}))=2(-a(x+1)-(b-a))=2(-ax-b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0\,.

It follows that

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D})
satisfies condition
(2) of Definition
1.2.7
.

Condition (3) of Definition
1.2.7
for
\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D})
follows from condition (3) of Definition
1.2.7
for
\mathfrak{D}
as
\psi(1)
is a proper map.
∎

Recall that we defined the scattering diagram
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in

section

1.4
. The following result expresses the fact that
\psi(1)
is a symmetry of

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Proposition 1.5.8
.

We have

\psi(1)(S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}))=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Proof.

We first remark that we have

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
.
Indeed, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\psi(1)(s_{n})=\psi(1)((n,-\frac{n^{2}}{2}))=(n+1,-\frac{n^{2}}{2}-n-\frac{1}{2})=(n+1,-\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{2})=s_{n+1}\,,

\mathrm{d\psi}(m_{n}^{-})=\mathrm{d\psi}((1,-n))=(1,-n-1)=m_{n+1}^{-}\,,

\mathrm{d\psi}(m_{n}^{+})=\mathrm{d\psi}((-1,n))=(-1,n+1)=m_{n+1}^{+}\,,

and so, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every
integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, we have

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{+})=\mathfrak{d}_{n+1,\ell}^{+}
and

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\ell}^{-})=\mathfrak{d}_{n+1,\ell}^{-}
.

The fact that
\psi(1)(S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}))=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
then follows from the uniqueness of the consistent completion given by Proposition

1.3.8
.
∎

2.
The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

In
section

2.1
we fix some notation for coherent sheaves on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
In
section

2.2
we review Bridgeland stability conditions on the derived category of coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and we introduce a special set of coordinates
on a particular slice of the space
of these stability conditions.
In
section

2.3
we review
properties of moduli spaces of Bridgeland semistable objects.
In
section

2.4

we introduce numerical invariants of these moduli spaces, in particular Hodge numbers of the intersection cohomology.
In
section

2.5
, we
use these numerical invariants to
construct a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
.
In
section

2.6
we establish a symmetry property of the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.

2.1.
Coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{2}

Let
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
be the abelian category of coherent sheaves
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Given
E
a coherent sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we denote

r(E)
its rank,
d(E)
its degree,
\chi(E)\coloneq\dim H^{0}(E)-\dim H^{1}(E)+\dim H^{2}(E)
its Euler characteristic, and

\gamma(E)\coloneq(r(E),d(E),\chi(E))\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\,.

We call
\gamma(E)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
the class of
E
. Remark that
E\mapsto\gamma(E)
is additive:
for every
E
and
F
coherent sheaves on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we have
\gamma(E\oplus F)=\gamma(E)+\gamma(F)
.
The data of
\gamma(E)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
is equivalent to the data of the Chern character of
E
.
Indeed, we have

\operatorname{ch}_{0}(E)=r(E)\,,\operatorname{ch}_{1}(E)=d(E)\,,\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E)=\chi(E)-r(E)-\frac{3}{2}d(E)\,.

Let
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
be the bounded derived category of
the abelian category
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. We denote

\Gamma\coloneqq K_{0}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))=K_{0}(\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))\,.

We have

\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{3}\,

[E]\mapsto\gamma(E)=(r(E),d(E),\chi(E))\,.

For every object
E
of

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, we denote
\gamma(E)
its class in

\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
We denote

\Gamma^{0}\coloneq\{(0,0,\chi)|\,\chi\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset\Gamma\,.

We have
\Gamma^{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. If
E
is a coherent sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, then

\gamma(E)\in\Gamma^{0}
if and only if
E
is supported in dimension
0
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(n)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. For every object
E
in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, we denote

E(n)\coloneq E\otimes E(n)
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

We denote

(-,-)\colon\Gamma\otimes\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\,,

the bilinear form on
\Gamma
given by,
for
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
and

\gamma^{\prime}=(r^{\prime},d^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
,

(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\coloneq-3dr^{\prime}-rr^{\prime}-dd^{\prime}+r\chi^{\prime}+\chi r^{\prime}\,.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

The bilinear form
(-,-)
on

\Gamma
coincides with the Euler form on

\Gamma=K_{0}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
, that is,
for every objects
E
and
E^{\prime}
of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, the Euler characteristic

\chi(E,E^{\prime})\coloneq\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E,E^{\prime})-\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,E^{\prime})+\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E^{\prime})\,

is given by

\chi(E,E^{\prime})=(\gamma(E),\gamma(E^{\prime}))\,.

Proof.

Let
E
and
E^{\prime}
be two objects of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. We denote
\gamma(E)=(r,d,\chi)
and
\gamma(E^{\prime})=(r^{\prime},d^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
.
By the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula, we have, denoting
H=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))
,

\chi(E,E^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\operatorname{ch}(E^{\vee})\operatorname{ch}(E^{\prime})\operatorname{td}(\mathbb{P}^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(r-dH+\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E))(r^{\prime}+d^{\prime}H+\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E^{\prime}))(1+\frac{3}{2}H+H^{2})

=rr^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}(rd^{\prime}-dr^{\prime})+r\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E^{\prime})+\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E)r^{\prime}-dd^{\prime}\,.

Using that
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E)=\chi-r-\frac{3}{2}d
and
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(E^{\prime})=\chi^{\prime}-r^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2}d^{\prime}
, we get the desired formula.
∎

Lemma 2.1.3
.

For every
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we have

(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})-(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)=\langle m_{\gamma},m_{\gamma}^{\prime}\rangle\,.

Proof.

Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
. We denote

\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
and
\gamma^{\prime}=(r^{\prime},d^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
. We recall from Definition

2.5.3
that
m_{\gamma}=(r,-d)
,

m_{\gamma^{\prime}}=(r^{\prime},-d^{\prime})
, and from

section

1.3
that

\langle(a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle=3(a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime})\,.

Therefore, we have

\langle m_{\gamma},m_{\gamma^{\prime}}\rangle=3(rd^{\prime}-dr^{\prime})\,.

On the other hand, using Definition
2.1.1
, we have

(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})-(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)=3(rd^{\prime}-dr^{\prime})\,.

∎

The skew-symmetric bilinear form
\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})-(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)

on
\Gamma
can be viewed as the Euler form of the Calabi-Yau-3 category
\operatorname{D}^{b}_{0}(K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
of coherent sheaves on
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
set-theoretically
supported on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

2.2.
Stability conditions

We first recall the classical notions of

\mu
-stability and Gieseker stability.
We refer to
[
HL10
]

for details.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
E
be a coherent sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
r(E)\neq 0
. The

slope
of
E
is

\mu(E)\coloneq\frac{d(E)}{r(E)}\,.

Definition 2.2.2
.

A coherent sheaf
E
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is
\mu
-semistable
(respectively
\mu
-stable
) if

E
is purely of dimension 2 (that is; the dimension of every nonzero subsheaf of
E
is
2
), and, for every nonzero strict subsheaf
F
of
E
, we have
\mu(F)\leqslant\mu(E)

(respectively
\mu(F)<\mu(E)
).

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
E
be a coherent sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The reduced Hilbert polynomial is the monic polynomial

p_{E}(n)\coloneq\frac{\chi(E(n))}{\alpha_{E}}\,,

where
\alpha_{E}
is the leading coefficient of the Hilbert polynomial

\chi(E(n))
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

A coherent sheaf
E
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is

Gieseker semistable
(respectively
stable
) if
E
is of pure dimension
(that is, every nonzero subsheaf of
E
has a support of dimension equal to the dimension of the support of
E
), and, for every nonzero strict subsheaf
F

of
E
, we have
p_{F}(n)\leqslant p_{E}(n)

(respectively
p_{F}(n)<p_{E}(n)
) for
n
large enough.

We now recall the notion stability condition
in the sense of Bridgeland
[
Bri07
]
,
in the particular case of the triangulated category
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Definition 2.2.5
.

A
prestability condition

\sigma
on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
consists
of a pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
, such that:

•

\mathcal{A}
is the heart of a bounded t-structure on

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

•

Z
is a linear map

Z\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, called the
central charge
.

•

For every nonzero object
E
of
\mathcal{A}
, we have

Z(E)=\rho(E)e^{i\pi\phi(E)}
with
\rho(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
and
0<\phi(E)\leqslant 1
, that is
Z(E)
is contained in the upper half-plane minus the nonnegative real axis.

•

A nonzero object
F
of
\mathcal{A}
is
\sigma
-semistable
if for every nonzero subobject
F^{\prime}
of
F
in
\mathcal{A}
, we have
\phi(F^{\prime})\leqslant\phi(F)
.
We require the Harder-Narasimhan property, that is, that every nonzero object
E
of
\mathcal{A}
admits a finite filtration

0\subset E_{0}\subset E_{1}\dots\subset E_{n}=E

in
\mathcal{A}
, with each factor
F_{i}\coloneq E_{i}/E_{i-1}

\sigma
-semistable
and
\phi(F_{1})>\phi(F_{2})>\dots>\phi(F_{n})
.

Definition 2.2.6
.

Given
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, an object

F
of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is called
\emph{$\sigma$-stable}
if
F

is a nonzero object of
\mathcal{A}
, and, for every nonzero strict subobject
F^{\prime}
of
F
in
\mathcal{A}
, we have
\phi(F^{\prime})<\phi(F)
.

Definition 2.2.7
.

A stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is a prestability condition
satisfying the
support property
, that is, such that there exists a quadratic form
Q
on the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\Gamma\otimes\mathbb{R}

such that:

•

The kernel of
Z
in
\Gamma\otimes\mathbb{R}
is negative
definite with respect to
Q
,

•

For every
\sigma
-semistable object, we have

Q(\gamma(E))\geqslant 0
.

Remarks:

•

If
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
is a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

which satisfies the support property, then the image by
Z\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

of the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that there exists a
\sigma
-semistable
object of class
\gamma
is discrete in
\mathbb{C}
.

•

The support property is a notion due to Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS08
, §1.2]
.
We refer to

[
BM11
, Appendix B]
for a comparison with earlier notions of finiteness for stability conditions.

We denote
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
the set of stability conditions on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
According to
[
Bri07
]
,
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
has a natural structure of complex manifold of dimension
3
, such that the map

\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}

\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\mapsto Z

is a local isomorphism of complex manifolds
(locally on
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
).

Following
[
Bri08
,
AB13
,
BM11
,
ABCH13
]
,
we review a standard way to construct examples of stability conditions on

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Definition 2.2.8
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote:

•

\operatorname{Coh}^{\leqslant s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
the subcategory of

\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
generated (by extensions) by

\mu
-semistable sheaves of slope
\leqslant s
.

•

\operatorname{Coh}^{>s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
the subcategory of
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

generated (by extensions) by
\mu
-semistable sheaves of slope

>s
and torsion sheaves.

•

\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
the subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

of objects
E
such that
\mathcal{H}^{i}(E)=0
for
i\neq-1,0
,

\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E)
is an object of
\operatorname{Coh}^{\leqslant s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
,
and
\mathcal{H}^{0}(E)
is an object of
\operatorname{Coh}^{>s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

The category
\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is obtained from

\operatorname{Coh}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
by tilt of the torsion pair

(\operatorname{Coh}^{>s}(\mathbb{P}^{2}),\operatorname{Coh}^{\leqslant s}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))\,.

In particular,
\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}s}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is an abelian category and the heart of a bounded
t-structure on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Definition 2.2.9
.

For every
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
t>0
, let
\tilde{Z}^{(s,t)}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

be the linear map defined

\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\mapsto\tilde{Z}_{\gamma}^{(s,t)}\,,

\tilde{Z}_{\gamma}^{(s,t)}\coloneq-\frac{r}{2}(s+it)^{2}+d(s+it)+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi\,.

We have

\tilde{Z}_{\gamma}^{(s,t)}=r\left(-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2})-ist\right)+d(s+it)+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi

=-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2})r+ds+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi+i(d-sr)t\,.

If
E
is an object of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of class
\gamma(E)\in\Gamma
, then we can write

\tilde{Z}_{\gamma(E)}^{(s,t)}=-\int_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}e^{-(s+it)H}\operatorname{ch}(E)\,,

where
H\coloneq c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))
.

According to
[
Bri08
,
AB13
,
BM11
]
, for every

(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
t>0
, the pair

(\tilde{Z}^{(s,t)},\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}s}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))

is a stability condition on

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. In particular, we get
an embedding of the upper half-plane

\{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,t>0\}
into

\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

We now do something new, which is to describe the same slice inside
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, but using coordinates
(x,y)
different from the usual coordinates
(s,t)
. This will replace the upper half-plane

\{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,t>0\}
by the
‘upper parabola’
U=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}
, which already appeared in
section

1.3
.

Definition 2.2.10
.

For every
(x,y)\in U=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}
,
let
Z^{(x,y)}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the linear map defined by

\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\mapsto Z_{\gamma}^{(x,y)}\,,

Z_{\gamma}^{(x,y)}\coloneqq ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi+i(d-rx)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,.

Proposition 2.2.11
.

For every
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
, the pair

(Z^{(x,y)},\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}x}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))

is a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Proof.

According to
[
Bri08
,
AB13
,
BM11
]
, for every
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
t>0
, the pair

(\tilde{Z}^{(s,t)},\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}s}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))

is a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

The map

(s,t)\mapsto(s,-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2}))

defines a bijection between the upper half-plane

\{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,t>0\}

and

U=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|\,y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}\,,

of inverse

\psi\colon(x,y)\mapsto(x,\sqrt{x^{2}+2y})\,,

such that
Z^{(x,y)}=\tilde{Z}^{\psi(x,y)}
.
∎

Remarks:

•

Proposition
2.2.11
gives a map

U\rightarrow\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})\,

\sigma=(x,y)\mapsto(Z^{(x,y)},\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}x}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))\,

which is injective.
Viewing
U
as an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
, this map is holomorphic. From now on, we use the same notation
\sigma
for a point

\sigma=(x,y)
in
U
or for the corresponding stability condition

\sigma=(Z^{\sigma},\mathcal{A}^{\sigma})\coloneq(Z^{(x,y)},\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}x}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
.

•

If
E
is a skyscraper sheaf
k(p)
,
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, then

\gamma(E)=(0,0,1)
and
Z_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma}=-1
for every
\sigma\in U
.

•

The choice of the coordinates

(x,y)
instead of the more traditional
coordinates
(s,t)
is motivated by the fact that the real part of the central charge,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi\,,

is affine in
(x,y)
(but quadratic in
(s,t)
). This will be the key point enabling us to make contact with scattering diagrams
in
section

2.5
.

2.3.
Moduli spaces and walls

For every
\sigma\in U
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we denote

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
the moduli stack of
\sigma
-semistable
objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of class
\gamma
.
According to
[
ABCH13
]
, the Artin stack
\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
admits a good
moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
which is in fact a projective scheme whose
closed points are in one-to-one correspondence with

S
-equivalence classes of
\sigma
-semistable objects of class

\gamma
. This follows from a description of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}

as moduli space of quiver representations,
see

[
ABCH13
, Proposition 7.5]
.
The moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
of
\sigma
-stable objects of class

\gamma
is a quasiprojective scheme, open in
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
.

According to
[
LZ19b
]
, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)=0
for every
\sigma
-semistable object
E
with
\gamma(E)\notin\Gamma^{0}
, and so the stacks

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
of
\sigma
-semistable objects and the moduli spaces

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
of
\sigma
-stable objects are smooth if

\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
.

If
E
is a
\sigma
-semistable object of class
\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
, then,
using that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)=0
and Lemma
2.1.2
,
we find that the dimension of

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
at
E
is

\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,E)-\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{0}(E,E)=-(\gamma,\gamma)
. In short, if

\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
, the moduli stack

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is smooth and equidimensional of dimension

\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=-(\gamma,\gamma)\,.

If
E
is a
\sigma
-stable object of class
\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
, then,
using that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)=0
, that
\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{0}(E,E)=1

as a stable object is simple, and Lemma
2.1.2
,
we find that the dimension of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
at
E
is
\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,E)=1-\chi(E,E)=1-(\gamma,\gamma)
.
In short, if
\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
, the moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
is smooth and equidimensional of dimension

\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=1-(\gamma,\gamma)\,.

In fact,
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
is also smooth and equidimensional if
\gamma\in\Gamma^{0}
, as it is

\mathbb{P}^{2}
if
\gamma=(0,0,1)
, and the emptyset else (this follows from Lemma 6.3 and Lemma 10.1 of
[
Bri08
]
).
The moduli spaces

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
of
\sigma
-semistable objects are singular in general.

Our main interest is in the study of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
as a function of

\sigma
.
Such study, mainly from the birational point of view, has been done in

[
ABCH13
,
BMW14
,
CH16
,
CH14
,
CH15
,
CHW17
,
Hui16
,
LZ18
,
LZ19a
,
Mar17
,
Ohk10
,
Woo13
]
.
The result is that, for a fixed
\gamma
, there are finitely many
curves in
U
, called
actual walls
for
\gamma
, such that if
\sigma

moves in a given connected component of the complement of the walls in
U
,
then
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
does not change. In other words,

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
, as a function of
\sigma
, only changes when

\sigma
crosses an actual wall.

Every
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
not collinear with
\gamma
defines a
potential wall

W_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
for
\gamma
, defined
as the set of
\sigma\in U
such that
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
and
Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma}

are positively collinear. It follows from the explicit formula giving

Z^{\sigma}
, Definition
2.2.10
, that each potential wall is either a parabola or a vertical
line in
U
. Every actual wall for
\gamma
is contained in a potential wall for
\gamma
.
Not every potential wall for
\gamma
is an actual wall for
\gamma
: there are only
finitely many actual walls for
\gamma
but in general infinitely many potential walls for
\gamma
.
An algorithm to determine the actual walls for
\gamma
is presented in
[
LZ19a
]
.

We will not be interested in the study of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
as a function of
\sigma
from the birational point of
view, but from the point of view of numerical cohomological invariants:
Euler characteristics, Betti numbers, Hodge numbers. We will consider these
invariants for intersection cohomology rather than for singular cohomology:
as the moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
are in general singular, we will obtain invariants with better properties this way.

2.4.
Intersection cohomology invariants

We refer to
[
BBD82
,
dCM09
,
Sai90
]
, for general notions on intersection cohomology, perverse sheaves and mixed Hodge modules, and to
[
MR17
,
Mei15
]

for applications in the context of Donaldson-Thomas theory, which will be ultimately relevant for us.

We recall that given
X
a projective variety,
Z
an equidimensional subvariety of

X
, and
L
a variation of pure Hodge structures on an open subset
Z^{\circ}

of the smooth part of
Z
, then there is a canonical pure Hodge module

IC_{Z}(L)
on
X
such that
IC_{Z}(L)|_{Z^{\circ}}=L
.

For every
\sigma\in U
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we apply this construction to

X=M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
,
Z=\overline{M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}}
the closure
of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
in
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
,
Z^{\circ}=M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
, and

L=\mathbb{Q}
viewed as a trivial variation of pure Hodge structures of type
(0,0)
.
Remark that

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}
is equidimensional by
section

2.3
.
We obtain a mixed Hodge module
IC_{\overline{M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}}}(\mathbb{Q})

on
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
.
By Saito theory of mixed Hodge modules
[
Sai90
]
, for every

k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the cohomology group

IH^{k}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma},\mathbb{Q})\coloneq H^{k}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma},IC_{\overline{M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}}}(\mathbb{Q}))

is naturally a pure Hodge structure of weight
k
,
and so has Hodge numbers, that we denote
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})
.
We organize these intersection Hodge numbers into some signed symmetrized intersection
Hodge polynomial

Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\coloneq(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}\sum_{p,q=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}(-1)^{p+q}Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})u^{p}v^{q}\in\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},v^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]\,.

We organize the intersection Betti numbers

Ib_{j}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})\coloneq\dim H^{j}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma},IC_{\overline{M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}}}(\mathbb{Q}))=\sum_{p+q=j}Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})\,,

into some signed symmetrized intersection
Poincaré polynomial

Ib_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})\coloneq(-q^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}\sum_{j=0}^{2\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}(-1)^{j}Ib_{j}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})q^{\frac{j}{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]\,.

Finally, we can consider the intersection Euler characteristic

Ie_{\gamma}^{+,\sigma}\coloneq\sum_{j=0}^{2\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}(-1)^{j}Ib_{j}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

and its signed version

Ie_{\gamma}^{-,\sigma}\coloneq(-1)^{\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}\sum_{j=0}^{2\dim M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}(-1)^{j}Ib_{j}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

We have the obvious specialization relations:

Ib_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})=Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{1}{2}})\,,

Ie_{\gamma}^{-,\sigma}=Ib_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(q^{\frac{1}{2}}=1)\,.

If
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
, which happens for
example if
\gamma
is a primitive element of the lattice

\Gamma
, then
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is a smooth projective variety and the intersection Hodge numbers, Betti numbers, Euler characteristic of

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
coincide with the usual Hodge numbers, Betti numbers,
Euler characteristic of
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
.

2.5.
Scattering diagrams from stability conditions

.

Recall that we introduced in

section

1.3
the skew-symmetric form
\langle-,-\rangle
on
M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\langle(a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle=3(a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime})\,.

In this section we use the intersection Hodge polynomials

Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
the intersection Poincaré polynomials
Ib_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
and the intersection Euler characteristics

Ie_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
, defined in
section

2.4
,
to construct scattering diagrams on
U
, in the sense of
Definition
1.2.7
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{q^{\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{\pm}}
, respectively for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
,
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{q^{\pm}})
,
and

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{\pm}})
. We recall that the Lie algebras

\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{\pm}}
have been defined in

section

1.4
.

For every
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\in\Gamma
, we define

L_{\gamma}\coloneq\{\sigma\in U|\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}\,.

Using the explicit formula for
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
given in Definition

2.2.10
, we find that

L_{\gamma}=\{(x,y)\in U|\,ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi=0\,,-rx+d>0\}\,.

Remark that
L_{\gamma}=L_{\gamma^{\prime}}
if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}

are positively collinear in
\Gamma
.

More explicitly, we have
the following cases according to the sign of
r
and
d
:

•

If
r>0
, then
L_{\gamma}
is the intersection of the line of
equation

ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi=0

with
U
and with the right half-plane
x<\frac{d}{r}
.

•

If
r<0
, then
L_{\gamma}
is the intersection of the line of equation

ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi=0

with
U
and with the left half-plane

x>\frac{d}{r}
.

•

If
r=0
and
d>0
, then
L_{\gamma}
is the intersection of the vertical line of equation

x=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{d}

with
U
.

•

If
r=0
and
d\leqslant 0
, then
L_{\gamma}
is empty.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we denote
\bar{L}_{\gamma}
the closure of
L_{\gamma}
in
\bar{U}
.

Definition 2.5.1
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we define

R_{\gamma}\coloneqq\{\sigma\in U\,|\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,,Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\neq 0\}\,.

By definition, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
R_{\gamma}
is a subset of
L_{\gamma}
.
We denote
\bar{R}_{\gamma}
the closure of
R_{\gamma}
in
\bar{U}
: it is a subset of

\bar{L}_{\gamma}
.

Definition 2.5.2
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we denote

\Gamma_{\gamma}\coloneq\{\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma\,|\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\text{for some }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}\}\,,

that is,
\Gamma_{\gamma}
is the finite set of elements in the lattice
\Gamma

dividing
\gamma
.

The moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
, and so the intersection Hodge
polynomials
Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
, are constant
as a function of
\sigma
for
\sigma
lying in a given connected component of the complement in
U
of the finitely many actual
walls for
\gamma
. It follows that, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have a natural decomposition

R_{\gamma}=\bigcup_{j\in J_{\gamma}}R_{\gamma,j}\,,

where:

•

J_{\gamma}
is the finite set indexing connected
components of
R_{\gamma}
minus the intersection points with the finitely many actual walls where there exists
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}
such that

Ih_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
jumps.

•

R_{\gamma,j}
is the closure in
R_{\gamma}
of the corresponding connected
component indexed by
j\in J_{\gamma}
. We denote
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
the closure of
R_{\gamma,j}
in

\bar{U}
.

Each
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
is either a bounded line segment contained in
\bar{L}_{\gamma}

or a half-line contained in
\bar{L}_{\gamma}
.

For every
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}
, the interior of each
R_{\gamma,j}
is away from the actual walls for

\gamma^{\prime}
, so the moduli spaces
M_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma}
and
M_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}

are isomorphic if
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are two points in the interior of some

R_{\gamma,j}
. We denote
M_{\gamma^{\prime},j}
the corresponding moduli space,

Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
the corresponding intersection Hodge
polynomial,

Ib_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})
the corresponding Poincaré polynomial, and

Ie_{\gamma^{\prime},j}^{-}

the corresponding signed Euler characteristic.

Definition 2.5.3
.

For every
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\in\Gamma
, we denote

m_{\gamma}\coloneq(r,-d)\in M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Remark that, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
the integral vector
m_{\gamma}
is a direction for
L_{\gamma}
.

If
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
is a bounded line segment, then there exists exactly
one endpoint of this line segment, that we denote

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j})
, and one positive real number, that we denote

T_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}
, such that

\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}]m_{\gamma}\,.

If
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
is a half-line, we denote
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j})

its endpoint, and it follows from the explicit description of
L_{\gamma}
that we have

\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j})-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\gamma}\,.

It follows that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
j\in J_{\gamma}
, we can naturally view

\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
as a naked ray of class
m_{\gamma}
in
U
in the sense of Definition

1.2.1
. We denote
|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}|
this naked ray of class
m_{\gamma}
.

Definition 2.5.4
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma

and
j\in J_{\gamma}
, we denote
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}
the ray of class

m_{\gamma}
in
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
, given by

(|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}})
,
where

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in(\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})_{m_{\gamma}}\,.

Definition 2.5.5
.

We denote
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
the collection of rays

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}
, where
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

j\in J_{\gamma}
.

Proposition 2.5.6
.

The collection of rays
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is a scattering diagram on

U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})

in the sense of Definition
1.2.7
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
and let
m\in M
.
Let
\mathfrak{d}
be a ray of

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
of class
m
and such that
\sigma
belongs to the interior of

|\mathfrak{d}|
. We have
\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
j\in J_{\gamma}
. The class
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
is uniquely determined by the condition

m_{\gamma}=(r,-d)=m
(using Definition
2.5.3
) and the condition

\sigma\in L_{\gamma}
, that is,

\chi=ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d
. The element
j\in J_{\gamma}
is uniquely determined by the fact that

\sigma
belongs to the interior of
|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}|
and that the interiors of
|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j_{1}}|
and

|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j_{2}}|
are distinct for

j_{1},j_{2}\in J_{\gamma}
,
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.
It follows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

satisfies the first condition of Definition

1.2.6
. Given the form of

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}
, one can recover

Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
for

\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}
from
H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}
, and so, as

J_{\gamma}
is defined in terms of the jumps of
Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
also satisfies the second condition of
Definition
1.2.6
. In conclusion,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is normalized, that is,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
satisfies condition (1) of Definition
1.2.7
.

If
\sigma=(x,y)\in|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}|\cap U
, then, by definition of

L_{\gamma}
, we have

Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, so, using Definitions
1.2.4
and

2.2.10

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})=-rx+d=\frac{\operatorname{Im}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}}{\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}}>0\,.

It follows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
satisfies condition (2) of Definition
1.2.7
.

We fix
K
a compact set in
U
and some
k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
. As

K
is compact in
U
, there exists
C>0
such that
\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}<C
for every
(x,y)\in K
. In particular, if

\sigma=(x,y)\in K
, then, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

\operatorname{Im}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})

and so

\operatorname{Im}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}<C\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\,.

If
\sigma=(x,y)\in K
, then, the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is nonempty,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in[-1,1]
and
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k
is contained in the set
F_{\sigma}
of
\gamma\in\Gamma

such that
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is nonempty,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in[-1,1]
and

\operatorname{Im}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\leqslant Ck
, which is finite
according to the support property satisfied by the stability condition

\sigma
.

By definition of the topology on the space of stability conditions and by the support property, there exists an open subset
U_{\sigma}
of
K
containing
\sigma
such that
F_{\sigma^{\prime}}\subset F_{\sigma}
for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\sigma}
.
By compactness of
K
, there exists finitely many
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{l}
such that
K=\cup_{j=1}^{l}U_{\sigma_{j}}
. In particular, the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that there exists
\sigma\in K
such that

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is nonempty,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=0
and
\operatorname{Im}\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\leqslant Ck

is contained in
\cup_{j=1}^{l}F_{\sigma_{j}}
, which is finite. It follows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
satisfies condition (3)
of Definition
1.2.7
.
∎

Definition 2.5.7
.

We define similarly a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{q^{-}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, resp.
\mathfrak{D}_{q^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}})
, resp.
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}})
,
by repeating the previous construction with

R_{\gamma}\coloneqq\{\sigma\in U\,|\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,,Ib_{\gamma}^{\sigma}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})\neq 0\}\,,

and

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ib_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(q^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}})_{m_{\gamma}}\,,

resp.

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq(-1)^{(\gamma,\gamma)-1}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ib_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(q^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{q^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-q^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in(\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}})_{m_{\gamma}}\,.

Recall that we introduced the Euler bilinear form
(-,-)\colon\Gamma\otimes\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
in Definition

2.1.1
.

Definition 2.5.8
.

We define similarly a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
resp.
\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
,
on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})
, resp.
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
,
by repeating the previous construction with

R_{\gamma}\coloneqq\{\sigma\in U\,|\,Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,,Ie_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\neq 0\}\,,

and

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}Ie_{\gamma^{\prime},j}^{-}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})_{m_{\gamma}}\,,

resp.

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq(-1)^{(\gamma,\gamma)-1}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}Ie^{-}_{\gamma^{\prime},j}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})_{m_{\gamma}}\,.

2.6.
Action of
\psi(1)
on
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

In this section we establish a symmetry

\psi(1)
of the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
defined in

section

2.5
.

There is a natural action of the group of autoequivalences
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
on
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
(see
[
Bri07
, Lemma 8.2]
):
if
T\in\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
and

\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, the action of
T
on
\sigma
is defined by

T\cdot\sigma\coloneq(Z\circ T^{-1},T(\mathcal{A}))
.

In the remaining of this section we take
T=(-\otimes\mathcal{O}(1))\in\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
.
Recall that we defined in

section

1.5
an action

\psi(1)
on
U
.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

The action of
T
on
\operatorname{Stab}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

preserves
U
. Moreover, the action of
T

restricted to
U
on
U

coincides with the action of
\psi(1)

on
U
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
. We have to compute
T\cdot\sigma
.

T\cdot\sigma
.
We have
T(\mathcal{A}^{(x,y)})=T(\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}x}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))=\operatorname{Coh}^{\texttt{\#}x+1}(\mathbb{P}^{2})=\mathcal{A}^{\psi(1)((x,y))}
. Indeed, if
E
is a

\mu
-semistable sheaf of slope
x
, then

E(1)
is
\mu
-semistable of slope

\frac{d(E(1))}{r(E(1))}=\frac{d(E)+r(E)}{r(E)}=\frac{d(E)}{r(E)}+1=x+1\,.

Recall from Definition
2.2.10
that if

\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
, then

Z_{\gamma}^{(x,y)}\coloneqq ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi+i(d-rx)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,.

Using that

r(E(-1))=r(E)
,
d(E(-1))=d(E)-r(E)
,
\chi(E(-1))=\chi(E)+\frac{r(E)}{2}-\left(\frac{3}{2}r(E)+d(E)\right)
,
we find

T^{-1}(\gamma)=T^{-1}((r,d,\chi))=(r,d-r,\chi-r-d)\,.

Thus, we have

Z_{T^{-1}(\gamma)}^{(x,y)}=ry+(d-r)x+r+\frac{3}{2}(d-r)-\chi+r+d+i(d-r-rx)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}

=r(y-x+\frac{1}{2})+d(x+1)+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi+i(d-r(x+1))\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

and so, using Lemma
1.5.2
,
we have

Z_{T^{-1}(\gamma)}^{(x,y)}=Z_{\gamma}^{\psi(1)((x,y))}\,.

We conclude that
T\cdot\sigma=\psi(1)(\sigma)
.
∎

We recall that in

section

1.5

we also defined an operation

\psi(1)
on scattering diagrams
on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.

Proposition 2.6.2
.

We have

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})=\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.

Proof.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

is defined in terms of the moduli spaces

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
of
\sigma
-semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
for

\sigma\in U
. As

T\in\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2}))
, we have

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{T(\gamma)}^{T\cdot\sigma}
for every
\sigma\in U
and for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
According to Lemma
2.6.1
, we have

T\cdot\sigma=\psi(1)(\sigma)
for every
\sigma\in U
. It follows that

\psi(1)(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})=\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.
∎

3.
Consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

3.1.
Statement of Theorem
3.1.1

We introduced the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
in

section

2.5
.
The main result of the present section is the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
:

Theorem 3.1.1
.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
is consistent in the sense of Definition
1.2.11
.

The proof of Theorem
3.1.1
takes the remaining part of this section.
According to Definition

1.2.11
, we have to show that for every
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v})
, the local scattering diagram

(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
is consistent in the sense of Definition
1.1.8
.
Using the framework of

[
MR17
]
and

[
Mei15
]
, we will show that it is essentially a version of the wall-crossing
formula in Donaldson-Thomas theory.

We fix
\sigma=(x_{0},y_{0})\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
,
k
a nonnegative integer, and we will show that
the local scattering diagram

(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
is consistent at order
k
in the sense of Definition

1.1.7
.
By definition,
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
is a local scattering diagram in
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
identified with the tangent space to

U
at
\sigma
.

We start proving some general results on

(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
in

section

3.2
.
In

section

3.3
we review motivic numerical invariants which can be extracted from mixed Hodge theory. In
section

3.4
we use the Donaldson-Thomas theory framework of

[
MR17
]
and

[
Mei15
]

to prove Proposition
3.4.3
.
In
section

3.5
we prove Proposition

3.5.4
, which is a form of the wall-crossing formula in Donaldson-Thomas theory. Finally, we combine Proposition
3.4.3
and Proposition
3.5.4
to end the proof of Theorem

3.1.1
in
section

3.6
.

3.2.
Local structure near a wall

Recall from Definition
1.2.4
that
\varphi_{\sigma}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by
(a,b)\mapsto-ax-b
.
Recall from Definition
1.1.2
that a ray
\mathfrak{d}
of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
is outgoing if
|\mathfrak{d}|=-\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
, and ingoing if
|\mathfrak{d}|=\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Also, by Definition

1.1.4

of a local scattering diagram, we have
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>0
for every ray
\mathfrak{d}
of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
.
Thus, the ingoing rays of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
are contained
in the open half-plane

\varphi_{\sigma}>0
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
whereas the outgoing rays of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}

are contained in the open half-plane

\varphi_{\sigma}<0
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We label
\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,\mathfrak{d}_{K}^{\mathrm{in}}
the finitely many ingoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
with
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k
,
in such way that

\langle m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}},m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}}\rangle\leqslant 0

if

a\leqslant a^{\prime}
.
We label
\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,\mathfrak{d}_{L}^{\mathrm{out}}
the finitely many outgoing rays
\mathfrak{d}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\sigma}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}

with
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant k
, in such the way that

\langle m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}},m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}}\rangle\geqslant 0

if

b\leqslant b^{\prime}
.
The order
k
consistency of
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}

is then to equivalent to the equality of order
k
automorphisms
of
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}
:

\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{K}^{\mathrm{in}}}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{L}^{\mathrm{out}}}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}\,.

By definition,
(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}})_{\sigma}
is a local picture of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

at
\sigma
. It follows that, for every

a=1,\dots,K
(resp.
b=1,\dots,L
), there exists
unique

\gamma\in\Gamma
and
j\in J_{\gamma}
,
such that
\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}|
,

m_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}=m_{\gamma}
,
and

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}t^{\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})}

(resp.
m_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}=m_{\gamma}
, and

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}=H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}t^{\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})}
).
We denote
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq\gamma
and

j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq j

(resp.
\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}\coloneq\gamma

and
j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}\coloneq j
).

Lemma 3.2.1
.

The sublattice
\Gamma^{\sigma}
of
\Gamma
generated by
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
, and
\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}
,
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, is of rank
2
.

Proof.

As
\sigma\in U\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v})
, the sublattice
\Gamma^{\sigma}

has rank
\geqslant 2
.
As the lattice
\Gamma
is of rank
3
, it remains to show that the sublattice

\Gamma^{\sigma}

is not of rank 3.
As the rays
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
,
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
,

1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, meet at
\sigma
, all the central charges
Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma}
,

1\leqslant a\leqslant K
, and
Z_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma}
,

1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, are purely imaginary. If the sublattice
\Gamma^{\sigma}
had rank 3, we would conclude
that
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
is purely imaginary for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
But it cannot be the case as
Z_{(0,0,1)}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=-1
for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in U
,
see Remarks following Proposition
2.2.11
.
∎

The fact that all the rays
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K

and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
,

1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, meet at
\sigma
implies
that all the central charges
Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma}
,

1\leqslant a\leqslant K
, and
Z_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma}
,

1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, are purely imaginary, with positive imaginary parts, and
so in particular are positively collinear.
Therefore, there exists a potential wall
W_{\sigma}
passing through
\sigma
, characterized by the alignement of the central charges
Z_{\gamma}

for
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\sigma}
. According to Lemma
3.2.1
,
the lattice
\Gamma^{\sigma}
is of rank
2
, and so
W_{\sigma}
is the only potential wall passing through
\sigma
which is a potential wall for some
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\sigma}
.

By the local finiteness of actual walls
reviewed in
section

2.3
, there exists
U_{\sigma}
open convex neighborhood of
\sigma
in
U
such that every actual wall for any of

\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
,

\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
,
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, intersecting

U_{\sigma}
, necessarily coincides with

W_{\sigma}
in restriction to
U_{\sigma}
.
Up to shrinking further
U_{\sigma}
, we also assume that
W_{\sigma}

is the only potential wall intersecting
U_{\sigma}
of the form

W_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}},\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}}
,

1\leqslant a,a^{\prime}\leqslant K
, or

W_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}},\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}}
,

1\leqslant b,b^{\prime}\leqslant L
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

The tangent line to the wall
W_{\sigma}
at the point

\sigma=(x_{0},y_{0})
is the line in
U
of equation

(x-x_{0})x_{0}+(y-y_{0})=0\,.

In other words, identifying
M_{\mathbb{R}}
with the tangent
space to
U
at
\sigma
, it is the line of equation

\varphi_{\sigma}=0

Proof.

By definition of a wall, there exists

\gamma_{1}=(r_{1},d_{1},\chi_{1}),\gamma_{2}=(r_{2},d_{2},\chi_{2})\in\Gamma
with

r_{2}d_{1}-r_{1}d_{2}\neq 0
such that
W_{\sigma}
is defined by the condition that
Z_{\gamma_{1}}
and
Z_{\gamma_{2}}
are positively collinear. It follows from the explicit formula given in Proposition
2.2.10
for the central charge that
W_{\sigma}
is defined by the equation

F(x,y)=0
, where

F(x,y)=(d_{1}-r_{1}x)(r_{2}y+d_{2}x+r_{2}+\frac{3}{2}d_{2}-\chi_{2})-(d_{2}-r_{2}x)(r_{1}y+d_{1}x+r_{1}+\frac{3}{2}d_{1}-\chi_{1})\,,

and so the tangent line to
W_{\sigma}
in a point of coordinates

(x_{0},y_{0})
is given by the equation

(\partial_{x}F)(x_{0},y_{0})(x-x_{0})+(\partial_{y}F)(x_{0},y_{0})(y-y_{0})=0\,.

If we take

(x_{0},y_{0})=\sigma
, defined by the conditions

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{1}}=\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{2}}=0
, then we find the particularly simple formulas

(\partial_{x}F)(x_{0},y_{0})=(r_{2}d_{1}-r_{1}d_{2})x_{0}\,\,,(\partial_{y}F)(x_{0},y_{0})=r_{2}d_{1}-r_{1}d_{2}\,.

Using that
r_{2}d_{1}-r_{1}d_{2}\neq 0
, we deduce that the tangent line to
W_{\sigma}
at
\sigma
is given by the equation
(x-x_{0})x_{0}+(y-y_{0})=0
.
∎

It follows from Lemma
3.2.2
that all the rays

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{a},j^{\mathrm{in}}_{a}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
, are on one side of the wall

W_{\sigma}
and that all the rays

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{b},j^{\mathrm{out}}_{b}}
,
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
, are on the other side of the wall

W_{\sigma}
. Let
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}
be the connected component of

U_{\sigma}-(W_{\sigma}\cap U_{\sigma})
containing all
the rays
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{a},j^{\mathrm{in}}_{a}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
,
and let

U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{out}}
be the connected component of

U_{\sigma}-(W_{\sigma}\cap U_{\sigma})

containing all the rays
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{b},j^{\mathrm{out}}_{b}}
,
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
.

We choose a point
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\in U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}
between

W_{\sigma}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}
, and a point
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\in U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{out}}
between
W_{\sigma}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}
.
As there are only countably many potential walls, we can assume that

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
are away from every potential wall. Finally, as
according to Lemma
3.2.2
the tangent line to
W_{\sigma}
at
\sigma
is never vertical, we can assume that

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
have the same
x
-coordinates, that is, that the line passing through
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is vertical.

Figure
4
gives a schematic summary of the notation introduced above.

Figure 4.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

We have

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}<0
(resp.
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}<0
) for every
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
)

Proof.

The support of each ray
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
is contained in the line of equation

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}=0
.
Using that, if
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}=ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi
, and

m_{\gamma}=(r,-d)
, we see that the condition

\langle m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}},m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}}\rangle\leqslant 0\,\,\,(\text{resp}.\ \langle m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}},m_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}}\rangle\geqslant 0)\,,

if
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
(resp.
b\leqslant b^{\prime}
), is equivalent to the fact that the support of

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}

(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}}
)
is contained in the half-plane

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}\leqslant 0

(resp.
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{b^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}\leqslant 0
)
if

a\leqslant a^{\prime}
(resp.
b\leqslant b^{\prime}
). As
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}

(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
)
is between
W_{\sigma}
and

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}

(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}
), it follows that
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\in}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}<0

(resp.
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}<0
) for every
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).
∎

3.3.
Numerical invariants from mixed Hodge theory

In this section we review some definitions and facts which will be useful in the proof
Theorem
3.1.1
in
section

3.6
.

For every
X
quasiprojective variety over

\mathbb{C}
, the cohomology groups with compact support

H^{j}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
come with a natural
mixed Hodge structure

[
Del71
,
Del74
]
.
In particular, we have an increasing weight filtration
W
on
H^{j}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
and a decreasing Hodge filtration
F
on

H^{j}_{c}(X,\mathbb{C})
. For every

p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define virtual Hodge numbers

h^{p,q}_{\mathrm{vir}}(X)\coloneq\sum_{j=0}^{2\dim X}(-1)^{j}\dim\mathrm{Gr}_{F}^{p}\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{p+q}H^{j}_{c}(X,\mathbb{C})\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

If
X
is smooth and projective, the virtual Hodge numbers coincide with the usual Hodge numbers up to the sign
(-1)^{p+q}
.
We organize the virtual Hodge numbers in a virtual Hodge polynomial (also called
E
-polynomial):

h(X)(u,v)\coloneq\sum_{p,q}h^{p,q}_{\mathrm{vir}}(X)u^{p}v^{q}\in\mathbb{Z}[u,v]\,.

Setting
u=v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we get a virtual Poincaré polynomial:

b(X)(q^{\frac{1}{2}})=\sum_{j}b_{j,\mathrm{vir}}(X)q^{\frac{j}{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}}]\,,

where the virtual Betti numbers are
given by

b_{j,\mathrm{vir}}(X)=\sum_{p+q=j}h_{\mathrm{vir}}^{p,q}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

The key property that we will use is that the virtual Hodge polynomial is motivic, in the sense that:

•

If
Z
is a closed subvariety of a quasiprojective variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
, then

h(X)=h(Z)+h(X-Z)\,.

•

If
X
and
Y
are two quasiprojective varieties over
\mathbb{C}
, then

h(X\times Y)=h(X)h(Y)
.

The motivic property follows from the compatibility of the mixed Hodge structure with the excision long exact sequence
in cohomology with compact support and with the Künneth formula. The virtual Hodge polynomial

X\mapsto h(X)
is uniquely determined
by its values on the smooth projective varieties and by the motivic property.

According to
[
Toë05
, Theorem 3.10]

(see also
[
Joy07b
]
), the
virtual Hodge polynomial

X\mapsto h(X)\in\mathbb{N}[u,v]

with
X
quasiprojective variety over
\mathbb{C}
can be naturally extended
in a virtual Hodge rational function

X\mapsto h(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[u,v][(uv)^{-1},\{((uv)^{n}-1)^{-1}\}_{n\geqslant 1}]\subset\mathbb{Z}(u,v)\,,

with
X
Artin stack of finite type with
affine stabilizers.
Setting
u=v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we get a
virtual Poincaré rational function

X\mapsto b(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}}][q^{-1},\{(q^{n}-1)^{-1}\}_{n\geqslant 1}]\subset\mathbb{Z}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})\,.

We have for example, denoting
B\mathbb{C}^{*}
the classifying stack of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
,

h(B\mathbb{C}^{*})(u,v)=\frac{1}{uv-1}\,,

and

b(B\mathbb{C}^{*})(q^{\frac{1}{2}})=\frac{1}{q-1}\,.

If
X
is an Artin stack of finite type with affine stabilizers, writing
X
as a
finite
disjoint union of locally closed equidimensional substacks
X_{j}
, we define a symmetrized version of the virtual Hodge rational function by

\tilde{h}(X)(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\coloneq\sum_{j}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim X_{j}}h(X_{j})(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z}[u,v]((uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})\subset\mathbb{Z}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\,.

Setting
u=v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we get a symmetrized version

\tilde{b}(X)(q^{\frac{1}{2}})\in\mathbb{Z}(q^{\frac{1}{2}})
of the virtual Poincaré rational function.

We have for example, using that
\dim B\mathbb{C}^{*}=-1
,

\tilde{h}(B\mathbb{C}^{*})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})=-\frac{(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{uv-1}=-\frac{1}{(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\,,

and

\tilde{b}(B\mathbb{C}^{*})(q^{\frac{1}{4}})=-\frac{1}{q^{\frac{1}{2}}-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\,.

Beware that
X\mapsto\tilde{h}(X)
does not satisfy the same motivic property
that
X\mapsto h(X)
.

3.4.
Donaldson-Thomas formalism

In this section we prove Proposition

3.4.3
, which will be used in the the proof of
Theorem
3.1.1

in
section

3.6
. We use
systematically the notation introduced in
section

3.2
.

Let

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
be the abelian category heart of the stability condition
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
be the abelian category heart of the stability condition

\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
.
As we have chosen
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
with the same
x
-coordinate, it follows from Definition
2.2.8
that
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
. In what follows, we denote
\mathcal{A}
this abelian category.

For every nonzero
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in U
, we denote

\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\,.

In particular, we have
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}
if and only if
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Shrinking
U_{\sigma}
if necessary, it follows from the support property for stability conditions that there exists an interval
I\subset(0,1)
containing
\frac{1}{2}

and closed in
[0,1]
such that:

•

The set of
\gamma\in\Gamma

such that
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k

and
\phi^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\gamma}\in I

does not depend on
\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\sigma}
.
We denote
\Lambda
the union of this finite set with
\{0\}

(which depends on
\sigma
and
k
).

•

For every

\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\sigma}
,
the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma

such that
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k
,
\phi^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\gamma}\in I

and
Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})\neq 0
is contained in the set of

\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
,
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
,
and
\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
,
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
.

In general,
I
is a small interval around
\frac{1}{2}
.

Definition 3.4.1
.

For every
\phi\in I
, we denote

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
) the abelian subcategory of

\mathcal{A}
whose objects are
0
and the
nonzero
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
)-semistable objects
E

with

\phi_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi
(resp.
\phi_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi
).

The categories
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)

are indeed abelian by
[
Bri07
, Lemma 5.2]
.

As
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
) is away from every
potential wall, for every
\phi\in I
, the lattice of

\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi

(resp.
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi
) is of rank
1
. In particular, there exists
\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}\in\Gamma

(resp.
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}\in\Gamma
) such that

\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,|\,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi\,,\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\neq\emptyset\}\subset\mathbb{N}\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}\,

(resp.
\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,|\,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi\,,\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\neq\emptyset\}\subset\mathbb{N}\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}
)

5
5

5

The reader should not confuse the notation

\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}

(resp.
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}
),
defined for
\phi\in I
, with the notation

\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{a}
(resp.
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{b}
), defined for
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).
.

We apply the formalism of
