Donaldson-Thomas theory,
such as described by Meinhardt
[
Mei15
]
(see in particular Example 3.34 for a discussion of surfaces)
to the abelian categories
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)

and
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

For every
\phi\in I
, the abelian categories
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
and

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
satisfy the technical conditions
required to apply

[
Mei15
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Proof.

Technical conditions 1)-6) of
[
Mei15
]
are general assumptions about moduli spaces and deformation theory of objects in an abelian category. They follow for
the abelian categories

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
and

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)

from the fact that the moduli stacks and moduli spaces
of
\sigma^{\prime}
-semistable in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
have a description as moduli stacks and moduli spaces
of quiver representations for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in U
, see
[
ABCH13
, Corollary 7.6]
.

Technical condition 7) of
[
Mei15
]
is the smoothness of the moduli stacks of
objects, or equivalently the locally constant behaviour of

\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)-\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,F)
. As
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma^{0}

and for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in U
(see Remarks after Proposition

2.2.11
), and as
\varphi
is neither
0
or
1
, the class
\gamma
of an object in

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
or

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
is never in
\Gamma^{0}
.
Li-Zhao
[
LZ19b
]
have shown that, for every

\sigma^{\prime}\in U
, and for every
\sigma^{\prime}
-semistable objects
E
and
F

with
\phi_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=\phi_{\gamma(F)}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
and

\gamma(E),\gamma(F)\notin\Gamma^{0}
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,F)=0
.
It follows that
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
and

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
satisfy technical condition 7).

Technical condition 8) of
[
Mei15
]
is the symmetry of the pairing

\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)-\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,F)
. Let
E
and
F
be objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
(resp.

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
). Then we have seen in the check of technical condition 7)
that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,F)=0
. So we have

\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E,F)-\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,F)=\chi(E,F)\,,

which by the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula is given by

\chi(E,F)=\int_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\operatorname{ch}(E^{\vee})\operatorname{ch}(F)\operatorname{td}(\mathbb{P}^{2})\,.

As we have chosen
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
) away from every potential wall, the fact that
\phi_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi_{\gamma(F)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi

(resp.
\phi_{\gamma(E)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi_{\gamma(F)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi
)
implies that
\gamma(E)
and
\gamma(F)
, and so
\operatorname{ch}(E)
and
\operatorname{ch}(F)
, are collinear,
which by the above formula implies the symmetry
\chi(E,F)=\chi(F,E)
.
∎

The application of the Donaldson-Thomas formalism of
[
Mei15
]
to Gieseker
semistable sheaves on del Pezzo surfaces is discussed in

[
Mei15
, Example 3.34]
and in
[
MM18
]
. Lemma
3.4.2

will able us to apply this formalism to Bridgeland semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

Recall from
section

2.3
that for every

\gamma\in\Gamma
and

\sigma^{\prime}\in U
, the moduli stack

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
of

\sigma^{\prime}
-semistable objects of class

\gamma
in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is smooth.
The symmetrized virtual Hodge rational functions

\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma^{\prime}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
are defined according to
section

3.3
.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

For every
\phi\in I
,
we have the equality

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{n\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{nm_{\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}}}=\exp\left(-\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{n\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell nm_{\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}}}\right)\,,

of power series in
z^{m_{\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{\phi}}}
with coefficients in

\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
and the equality

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{n\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{nm_{\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}}}=\exp\left(-\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{n\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell nm_{\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}}}\right)\,,

of power series in
z^{m_{\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{\phi}}}
with coefficients in

\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Proof.

We apply

[
Mei15
, Theorem 1.1]
to

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(\phi)
.
The required assumptions have been checked in Lemma
3.4.2
.

More precisely, the main result of
[
Mei15
]

(Theorem 1.1 combined with the formula defining the Donaldson-Thomas invariants in
Lemma 5.1) is an equality between generating series with coefficients
in the Grothendieck group of mixed Hodge structures over
\mathbb{C}

with addition of a square root

\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}
of the Tate motive, and inversion of

\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}
,

(\mathbb{L}^{n}-1)
,
n\geqslant 1
.
We get numerical identities by application of the virtual Hodge function (extended such that
h(\mathbb{L}^{\frac{1}{2}})=-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}}
).
∎

3.5.
Wall-crossing formula

In this section, we prove Proposition

3.5.4
, which will be used in the the proof of
Theorem
3.1.1

in
section

3.6
.
We continue using the notation introduced in
section

3.2
–
section

3.4
.

By definition of the interval
I
, we have a finite subset
\Lambda
of
\Gamma
such that

\Lambda=\{0\}\cup\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,|\,\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k\,,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\in I\}=\{0\}\cup\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,|\,\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k\,,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\in I\}\,.

Definition 3.5.1
.

We denote
A_{\Lambda}
the associative noncommutative

\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
-algebra, given as a
\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
-vector space by

A_{\Lambda}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Lambda}\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\,,

and with the product defined by

z^{m_{\gamma}}\cdot z^{m_{\gamma^{\prime}}}=(-1)^{\langle m_{\gamma},m_{\gamma^{\prime}}\rangle}(uv)^{\frac{\langle m_{\gamma},m_{\gamma^{\prime}}\rangle}{2}}z^{m_{\gamma}+m_{\gamma^{\prime}}}

if
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}\in\Lambda
, and

z^{m_{\gamma}}\cdot z^{m_{\gamma^{\prime}}}=0

if
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}\notin\Lambda
.

Definition 3.5.2
.

For every
\phi\in I
, we define

\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq\{0\}\cup\{\gamma\in\Lambda\,|\,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\phi\}\,

and

\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}\coloneq\{0\}\cup\{\gamma\in\Lambda\,|\,\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=\phi\}\,.

We have

\Lambda=\bigcup_{\phi\in I}\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}=\bigcup_{\phi\in I}\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}\,.

Definition 3.5.3
.

We denote

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}

for an ordered product where the factors
with higher value of
\phi
are on the left of those with lower value of
\phi
.

Proposition 3.5.4
.

We have the following equality in
the
\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
-algebra
A_{\Lambda}
:

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\right)=\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\right)\,.

The proof of Proposition
3.5.4
takes the remaining part of

section

3.5
.

We follow the logic of the proof of

[
Joy08
, Proposition 6.20]
,
in which Joyce considers Gieseker semistable sheaves on a surface
S
with
K_{S}^{-1}
nef.
We refer to
[
Bri12
]
and

[
Joy07a
]
for details on definitions and on the use of motivic Hall algebras.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, let

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}
be the algebraic stack of objects of
\mathcal{A}
of class
\gamma
, and let

\mathfrak{M}\coloneq\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}\,.

Let
H(\mathfrak{M})
be the corresponding
motivic Hall algebra. Elements of
H(\mathfrak{M})
are motivic stack functions on
\mathfrak{M}
, that is, classes defined
up to scissor relations of

[Z\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}]
, with

Z
Artin stack of finite type with affine stabilizers (see
[
Bri12
]
). As a vector space, we have a
\Gamma
-grading

H(\mathfrak{M})=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma})\,,

where
H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma})
is the space of motivic stack functions supported on

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}
, that is of
[Z\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}]
factoring through
\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
.
The associative product
\star
on

H(\mathfrak{M})
is
\Gamma
-graded, that is,
has components, for every
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
,

\star\colon H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}})\otimes H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{2}})\rightarrow H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}})\,,

determined by pullback and pushforward to and from the stack of extensions in
\mathcal{A}
of objects of class
\gamma_{2}
by objects of class
\gamma_{1}
.

We denote

H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})\coloneq\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Lambda}H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma})\,

with product, for
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Lambda
,

\star\colon H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}})\otimes H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{2}})\rightarrow H(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}})\,,

restricted from
H(\mathfrak{M})
if
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\in\Lambda
, and set to
0
if
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\notin\Lambda
. This defines a structure of associative algebra on

H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})
.

For every
\gamma\in\Lambda
, the characteristic function of the stack

\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
)
of
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-semistable
(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
-semistable) objects in
\mathcal{A}
of class
\gamma
defines an element

\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}=[\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}]

(resp.
\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{out}}=[\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}]
) of
H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})
. For every
\phi\in I
, we define

\delta_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}}\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}\in H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})\,,

and

\delta_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}\coloneq\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{out}}\in H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})\,.

From the
existence and uniqueness of the Harder-Narasimhan filtration for the stability conditions
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
of heart
\mathcal{A}
, we have the identity

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\delta_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}=\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\delta_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}\,

in
H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})
.
If we denote
\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}
the values of
\phi\in I
such that
\delta_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}\neq 1\in H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})
, ordered such that

\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}>\dots>\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\,,

and
\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,\phi_{N^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}
the values of

\phi
such that

\delta_{\phi}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\neq 1\in H_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{M})
, ordered such that

\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}>\dots>\phi_{N^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}\,,

we can rewrite the above identity as

\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}}=\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}}\,.

Given the motivic property reviewed in

section

3.3
, we can apply the virtual Hodge polynomial
h
to an element
[Z\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}]
of the motivic Hall algebra
H(\mathfrak{M})
to obtain a

\mathbb{Z}(u,v)
-valued constructible function
h([Z\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}])
on
\mathfrak{M}
.
Recall that we introduced the bilinear Euler form
(-,-)\colon\Gamma\otimes\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
in Definition
2.1.1
.
Multiplying further by
(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma,\gamma)}
the restrictions to each component
\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}
,
we get an equality of
A_{\Lambda}
-valued constructible functions on

\mathfrak{M}
:

\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma,\gamma)}\,h\left((\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}

=\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma,\gamma)}\,h\left((\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,.

For every
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})\in\Lambda^{N}
, we denote

\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\gamma}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}

the stack of objects
E
with

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-Harder-Narasimhan factors

E_{1},\dots,E_{N}

of class
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N}
.
Similarly, for every
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N^{\prime}})\in\Lambda^{N^{\prime}}
, we denote

\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\gamma}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}

the stack of objects
E
with

\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
-Harder-Narasimhan factors

E_{1},\dots,

E_{N^{\prime}}

of class
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N^{\prime}}
.

As we have

\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma,\gamma)}\,h\left((\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\mathrm{in}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}

=\sum_{\underline{\gamma}\in\Lambda^{N}}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N})}\,h\left((\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{in}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\gamma}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N}}}\,,

and

\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma,\gamma)}\,h\left((\delta_{\phi_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\phi_{N^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\mathrm{out}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}

=\sum_{\underline{\gamma}\in\Lambda^{N^{\prime}}}(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N^{\prime}},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N^{\prime}})}\,h\left((\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{out}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{out}})|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\gamma}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}}\,,

Proposition
3.5.4
follows from the following Lemma
3.5.5
.

Lemma 3.5.5
.

For every
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})\in\Lambda^{N}
, we have an equality of
A_{\Lambda}
-valued constructible functions on

\mathfrak{M}
:

(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N})}\,h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{in}})z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N}}}

=\left((-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\sigma^{\mathrm{in}}}}\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots\left((-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{N}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}}\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{in}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{N}}}\,.

Similarly, for every
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N^{\prime}})\in\Lambda^{N^{\prime}}
, we have an equality of
A_{\Lambda}
-valued constructible functions on

\mathfrak{M}
:

(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N^{\prime}},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{N^{\prime}})}\,h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{out}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}^{\mathrm{out}})z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}}

=\left((-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}}\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{out}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots\left((-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}^{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}^{\mathrm{out}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma_{N^{\prime}}}}\,.

Proof.

We prove the formula for
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
. The proof of the formula for
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is formally identical.

According to
[
LZ19b
]
, for every

E

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-semistable object of class
\gamma
, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)=0\,,

and so, using Lemma
2.1.2
, we have

\dim\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E,E)-\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E,E)=-\chi(E,E)=-(\gamma,\gamma)\,.

On the other hand, by Definition

3.5.1
of the product in
A_{\Lambda}
, we have

z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots z^{m_{\gamma_{N}}}=(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\sum_{i<j}\langle\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}\rangle}z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N}}}\,,

which can be rewritten using Lemma

2.1.3

as

z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots z^{m_{\gamma_{N}}}=(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\sum_{i<j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})-\sum_{i>j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}z^{m_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+m_{\gamma_{N}}}\,.

Thus, given the identity

(\sum_{i}\gamma_{i},\sum_{j}\gamma_{j})=\sum_{i,j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})=\sum_{i}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i})+\sum_{i<j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})+\sum_{i>j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})

=\sum_{i}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i})+\left(\sum_{i<j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})-\sum_{i>j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})\right)+2\sum_{i>j}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})\,,

Lemma
3.5.5
follows from the following equality of

\mathbb{Z}(u,v)
-constructible functions on
\mathfrak{M}
:

h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{in}})=(uv)^{-\sum_{N\geqslant i>j\geqslant 1}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}})\dots h(\delta_{\gamma_{N}}^{\mathrm{in}})\,.

We prove by induction over
n
that, for every
1\leqslant n\leqslant N
, we have

h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}})=(uv)^{-\sum_{n\geqslant i>j\geqslant 1}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})}h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}})\dots h(\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}})\,.

The case
n=1
is trivial. Let us assume that the result is known for
n-1
and that we wish to prove it for
n
.
By existence and uniqueness of the

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-Harder-Narasimhan filtration, an object
E
in the support of
(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{in}})\star\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}}
can be uniquely written as
an extension

0\rightarrow F\rightarrow E\rightarrow E_{n}\rightarrow 0\,

with
E_{n}

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-semistable of class
\gamma_{n}
, and with
Y
of Harder-Narasimhan factors
E_{1},\dots,E_{n-1}
of class

\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1}
.
According to
[
LZ19b
]
, for every

G
and
G^{\prime}

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
-semistable objects with

\phi_{\gamma(G)}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}<\phi_{\gamma(G^{\prime})}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
, we have
6
6

6

Strictly speaking, this result is not stated in
[
LZ19b
]
but follows directly from the proofs given there.

\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(G,G^{\prime})=0\,.

In particular, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E_{n},E_{j})=0
, for every
1\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
, and as
F
is obtained by successive extensions of the
E_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
, we have also
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E_{n},F)=0
.

From the explicit description of the product in the motivic Hall algebra
(see
[
Bri12
, Proposition 6.2]

or
[
Joy07a
, Corollary 5.15]
), the fiber of
(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{n-1}})\star\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}}
at the point

(F,E_{n})
of
\mathfrak{M}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-1})}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma_{n}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
is given by

[\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{n},F)/\operatorname{Hom}(E_{n},F)]=[\mathbb{A}^{1}]^{\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{n},F)-\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E_{n},F)}\,.

As
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E_{n},F)=0
, we have, using
Lemma
2.1.2
,

\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{n},F)-\dim\operatorname{Hom}(E_{n},F)=-\chi(E_{n},F)=-(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{n-1})\,,

hence, using

h(\mathbb{A}^{1})=uv
, the desired relation

h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}})=(uv)^{-(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{n-1})}h(\delta_{\gamma_{1}}^{\mathrm{in}}\star\dots\star\delta_{\gamma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{in}})h(\delta_{\gamma_{n}}^{\mathrm{in}})\,.

∎

3.6.
End of the proof of Theorem
3.1.1

Lemma 3.6.1
.

We have the following equalities in the

\mathbb{Q}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
-algebra
A_{\Lambda}
:

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(-\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{\gamma}}\right)=\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant K}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}}\right)\,,

and

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(-\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{\gamma}}\right)=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant L}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}}\right)\,.

Proof.

As
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}

(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}}
)
are both
in
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{out}}
), and so are not separated by walls for
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}
(reps.
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}
), we have
M_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}=M_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}

(resp.
M_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}=M_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
), for every

\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\sigma}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\sigma}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}
).
By the construction of
I
, every
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\in I

(resp.
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\in I
)
and
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\gamma})\leqslant k
is of the form
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}
) for some
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).

It remains to show that the ordering according to decreasing value of

\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
(resp.
\phi_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
)
agrees with the ordering according to decreasing value of

1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).

We consider a small parametrized path
\alpha_{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\alpha_{\mathrm{out}}
)
in
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{out}}
) starting at
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}

(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
) and intersecting successively
the rays

\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
)
in the order of increasing
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).
According to Lemma
3.2.3
, we have

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\in}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}<0
(resp.
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{out}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}<0
) for every
1\leqslant a\leqslant K
(resp.
1\leqslant b\leqslant L
).
Recall that by definition of
U_{\sigma}
, there is no potential wall of the form

W_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}},\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}}}
,

1\leqslant a,a^{\prime}\leqslant K
,
(resp.
W_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}},\gamma_{\mathfrak{d}_{b^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{out}}}}
,

1\leqslant b,b^{\prime}\leqslant L
) intersecting
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}

(resp.
U_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{out}}
).
It follows that the relative ordering of the phases

\phi_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
coincides with the relative
ordering according to which
\alpha_{\mathrm{in}}
(resp.
\alpha_{\mathrm{out}}
) intersects
the rays
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}
).
∎

We can now end the proof of Theorem

3.1.1
.
According to
section

3.2
, we need to show the equality

\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{K}^{\mathrm{in}}}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}}=\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{L}^{\mathrm{out}}}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}}

of order
k
automorphisms
of
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}
.
By Definition
1.1.5
, the automorphism
\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}
attached to a ray

\mathfrak{d}
is given by

\Phi_{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq\exp([H_{\mathfrak{d}},-])\,.

As the commutator in the associative algebra
A_{\Lambda}
coincides with the Lie bracket in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}

(compare Definitions
3.5.1
and
1.4.1
), the above equality of automorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}_{u,v}[t^{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{k}
is equivalent to the following equality in
A_{\Lambda}
:

\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant K}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{in}},j_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}}\right)=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant L}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{out}},j_{b}^{\mathrm{out}}}}\right)\,.

According to Lemma
3.6.1
, this is equivalent to

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(-\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{\gamma}}\right)=\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\exp\left(-\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}}\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}z^{\ell m_{\gamma}}\right)\,,

which by Proposition
3.4.3
, is equivalent to

\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{in}}}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\right)=\prod_{\phi\in I}^{\rightarrow}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\phi}^{\mathrm{out}}}\tilde{h}(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}})(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})z^{m_{\gamma}}\right)\,.

But this last equality is exactly Proposition
3.5.4
, and this ends the proof of Theorem
3.1.1
.

4.
Initial data for
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}

In this section, we prove that the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
have in some sense the same initial data.
More precisely, we prove Theorem
4.2.1
, according to which the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
coincide in restriction to some neighbourhood

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
of the boundary of
U
.
We define
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
in

section

4.1
and we state
Theorem
4.2.1

in
section

4.2
.
We introduce a description of

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
in terms of quiver representations in

section

4.3
, which is then used in
section

4.4
to prove
Theorem
4.2.1
.

4.1.
The initial region
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}

Recall from
section

1.3
that for every

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we introduced naked rays

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|=s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{+}
and

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|=s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{-}
,
which were then used to define the initial scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the line segments

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
are contained
in the line of equation

y+nx-\frac{n^{2}}{2}=0\,,

that is,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{(x,y)}=0\,.

Proof.

The fact that
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
and
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
are contained
in the line of equation

y+nx-\frac{n^{2}}{2}=0

is immediate given that
|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|=s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{+}
and

|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|=s_{n}-[0,\frac{1}{2}]m_{n}^{-}
, with

s_{n}=(n,-\frac{n^{2}}{2})
,

m_{n}^{-}=(1,-n)
,

m_{n}^{+}=(-1,n)
.

It remains to show that
y+nx-\frac{n^{2}}{2}=0
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{(x,y)}=0
. Recall
from Definition

2.2.10
that, writing
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
, we have

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma}^{(x,y)}=ry+dx+r+\frac{3}{2}d-\chi\,.

On the other hand, we have

\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))=(1,n,\frac{n^{2}}{2}+\frac{3n}{2}+1)\,.

∎

Definition 4.1.2
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq\{\sigma=(x,y)\in\bar{U}|\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1))}^{\sigma}\leqslant 0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1))}^{\sigma}\leqslant 0\}

=\{(x,y)\in\bar{U}|y\leqslant-(n-1)x+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)^{2}\,,y\leqslant-(n+1)x+\frac{1}{2}(n+1)^{2}\}\,.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the boundary of

\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
is the union of:

•

The line segment
s_{n-1}-[0,1]m_{n-1}^{+}
, contained in the line of equation

y+(n-1)x-\frac{1}{2}(n-1)^{2}=0\,,

that is,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1)}^{\sigma}=0
.

•

The line segment

s_{n+1}-[0,1]m_{n+1}^{-}
, contained in the line of equation

y+(n+1)x-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)^{2}=0\,,

that is
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1)}^{\sigma}=0
.

•

The arc of the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, boundary of

\bar{U}
, delimited by the points
s_{n-1}
and
s_{n+1}
.

Remark that the line segments

s_{n-1}-[0,1]m_{n-1}^{+}
and

s_{n+1}-[0,1]m_{n+1}^{-}

intersect at the point

(n-1,-\frac{1}{2}(n-1)^{2})-(-1,n-1)=(n+1,-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)^{2})-(1,-(n+1))=(n,-\frac{1}{2}(n^{2}-1))\,,

intersection point of the lines
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1))}^{\sigma}=0
and

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1))}^{\sigma}=0
.

The line of equation

y+nx-\frac{n^{2}}{2}=0
, that is,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{\sigma}=0
, divides
\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
into three regions.
We have

\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}=\bar{U}_{n,T}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\bar{U}_{n,L}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\bar{U}_{n,R}^{\mathrm{in}}\,,

where:

•

\bar{U}_{n,T}^{\mathrm{in}}
is the triangle delimited by the three lines

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1))}^{\sigma}=0
,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{\sigma}=0
and

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1)}^{\sigma}=0
.
Remark that we have

\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{\sigma}=0\}\cap\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}=|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|\cup|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
.

•

\bar{U}_{n,L}^{\mathrm{in}}
is delimited
by the lines
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1))}^{\sigma}=0
,
\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}^{\sigma}=0
, and the arc of the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
delimited by the points

s_{n-1}
and
s_{n}
. Remark that we have

\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n-1))}^{\sigma}=0\}\cap\bar{U}_{n,L}^{\mathrm{in}}=|\mathfrak{d}_{n-1}^{+}|
and

\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}=0\}\cap\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{n,L}=|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{-}|
.

•

\bar{U}_{n,R}^{\mathrm{in}}
is delimited by the lines

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1))}^{\sigma}=0
,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n)}^{\sigma}=0
,
and the arc of the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
delimited by the points

s_{n}
and
s_{n+1}
. Remark that we have

\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n+1))}^{\sigma}=0\}\cap\bar{U}_{n,R}^{\mathrm{in}}=|\mathfrak{d}_{n+1}^{-}|
and

\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(n))}=0\}\cap\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{n,R}=|\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{+}|
.

Figure
5

gives a schematic summary of some of the notation introduced above.

Figure 5.

Definition 4.1.3
.

We denote

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}\coloneq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}\,.

Lemma 4.1.4
.

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a neighbourhood in

\bar{U}
of
the boundary
\partial\bar{U}=\{(x,y)|y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\}
of
\bar{U}
.

Proof.

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}

is a neighbourhood in

\bar{U}
of the arc of the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
defined by
n-1<x<n+1
.
The result follows from the fact that every point
(x,y)
of the parabola

y=-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
satisfies
n-1<x<n+1
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Equivalently, one can remark that

\{(x,y)\in\bar{U}|\,y<-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{8}\}\subset\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}\,.

∎

4.2.
Statement of Theorem
4.2.1

Theorem 4.2.1
.

The scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

coincide in restriction to
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

The proof of Theorem

4.2.1
takes the remaining part of

section

4
.

Recall that we defined an action

\psi(1)
on
U
in

section

1.5
.
We first remark that, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\psi(1)(\bar{U}_{n}^{\mathrm{in}})=\bar{U}_{n+1}^{\mathrm{in}}
. We also defined in

section

1.5
an action

\psi(1)
on scattering diagrams on
U
.
According to Proposition
1.5.8
,

\psi(1)
preserves
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

and according to Proposition

2.6.2
,
\psi(1)
also
preserves
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.
Therefore, in order to prove Theorem

4.2.1
, it is enough to show that the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

coincide in restriction to
\bar{U}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

We describe
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
in restriction to
\bar{U}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
It follows from Lemma
1.1.10
that
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

restricted to
\bar{U}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}
consists in the following rays:

•

For every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

\mathfrak{d}_{0,\ell}^{+}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{0}^{+}|,H_{0,\ell}^{+})
and

\mathfrak{d}_{0,\ell}^{-}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{0}^{-}|,H_{0,\ell}^{-})

(see
section

1.3
-
section

1.4
).

•

For every integer

\ell\geqslant 1
,

\mathfrak{d}_{-1,\ell}^{+}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{-1}^{+}|,H_{-1,\ell}^{+})
and

\mathfrak{d}_{1,\ell}^{-}=(|\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{-}|,H_{1,\ell}^{-})

(see
section

1.3
-

section

1.4
).

•

For every integer
\ell\geqslant 1
,

\mathfrak{d}_{-1,\ell}^{+,(1)}\coloneq\left(s_{-1}-[1/2,1]m_{-1}^{+},H_{-1,\ell}^{+}\right)\,,

and

\mathfrak{d}_{1,\ell}^{-,(1)}\coloneq\left(s_{1}-[1/2,1]m_{1}^{-},H_{1,\ell}^{-}\right)\,.

In order to prove
Theorem

4.2.1
,
it remains to show that

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
restricted to
\bar{U}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}
has an identical description.

4.3.
Quiver description

In this section we review a description of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
in terms of quiver representations, which will be used in the
proof of Theorem
4.2.1

in
section

4.4
.
A similar discussion can be found in

[
Ohk10
, §4.3]
7
7

7

To compare with the notation of
[
Ohk10
]
, recall that the exterior product

\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\otimes\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\rightarrow\wedge^{2}\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=\mathcal{O}(-3)
induces an isomorphism

T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\simeq\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(3)
.
.

We denote
T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
the tangent bundle of

\mathbb{P}^{2}
and we consider the strong exceptional collection

[
GR87
]

\mathcal{O}(1)\,,T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\,,\mathcal{O}(2)\,,

of objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
We denote

\mathbf{T}\coloneq\mathcal{O}(1)\oplus T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\oplus\mathcal{O}(2)\,.

It follows from the Beilinson spectral sequence
[
Bei78
]
that, introducing the algebra

A_{0}\coloneq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{T})^{\mathrm{op}}\,,

and
\mathcal{A}_{0}
the abelian category of
finitely generated left
A_{0}
-modules, the
functor

\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\,

E\mapsto\operatorname{RHom}(\mathbf{T},E)

is an equivalence of triangulated categories. Using this equivalence, we view

\mathcal{A}_{0}
as a subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

As

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(1),T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})=H^{0}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-1))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\,,

\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}},\mathcal{O}(2))=H^{0}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(2))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\,,

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(2))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(1))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\,,

the algebra
A_{0}
is the path-algebra of the quiver
Q_{0}
, consisting of three vertices
v_{-1}
,
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
, three arrows from
v_{-1}
to
v_{0}
, three arrows from

v_{0}
to
v_{1}
, and six linearly independent relations coming from the kernel of the composition map

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(1),T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}},\mathcal{O}(2))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(2))\,.

If we denote
\delta_{0}
,

\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
the three arrows from
v_{-1}
to
v_{0}
, and
\gamma_{0}
,

\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
the three arrows from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
, one can write the relations as

\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}+\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}=0
, for every
i,j=0,1,2
, see

[
Ohk10
, §4.3]
.

Q_{0}=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}

The abelian category
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is the category of finite-dimensional
representations of
Q_{0}
.
In particular, the dimension of a quiver representation, that is, the triple of dimensions of the vector spaces attached to the three vertices, defines an isomorphism

\dim\colon K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{3}\,,

such that
\dim([V])\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
if

V
is an object of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

The simple objects
S_{-1}
,
S_{0}
,
S_{1}

of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, one-dimensional representations of
Q_{0}
supported at the vertices

v_{-1}
,
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
of
Q_{0}
, of dimensions
(1,0,0)
,

(0,1,0)
,
(0,0,1)
,
correspond respectively to the objects

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]\,,\mathcal{O}[1]\,,\mathcal{O}(1)\,,

of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. The indecomposable
projective objects

P_{-1}
,
P_{0}
,
P_{1}
of

\mathcal{A}_{0}
, characterized by

\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(P_{j},S_{k})=\delta_{jk}\mathbb{C}
,
correspond respectively to the objects

\mathcal{O}(2)\,,T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\,,\mathcal{O}(1)\,,

of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
They correspond to quiver representations of dimension

(1,3,3)
,
(0,1,3)
,
(0,0,1)
respectively.
In
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we have natural projective resolutions of the simple objects:

0\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow S_{1}\rightarrow 0\,,

0\rightarrow P_{1}^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow P_{0}\rightarrow S_{0}\rightarrow 0\,,

0\rightarrow P_{1}^{\oplus 6}\rightarrow P_{0}^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow P_{-1}\rightarrow S_{-1}\rightarrow 0\,.

We now focus on the simple objects

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,
\mathcal{O}[1]
,
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
We can describe
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as the extension-closed
subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
generated by

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]\,,\mathcal{O}[1]\,,\mathcal{O}(1)
.
The only nonzero

\operatorname{Ext}
-groups between these objects are

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2],\mathcal{O}[1])=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(1))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\,,

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}[1],\mathcal{O}(1))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(1))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\,,

\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2],\mathcal{O}(1))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(2))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{6}\,.

In particular, the ordered collection

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]\,,\mathcal{O}[1]\,,\mathcal{O}(1)\,,

is Ext-exceptional in the sense of

[
Mac07
, Definition 3.10]
.
Therefore, by
[
Mac07
, Lemma 3.16]
,
if
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
is a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
such that

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,
\mathcal{O}[1]
,
\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to

\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

We denote by
K_{3}
the quiver with two vertices
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
and three arrows from

V_{1}
to
V_{2}
.

K_{3}=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2}}

One way to obtain the quiver
K_{3}
is to restrict the set of arrows of
Q_{0}
to those starting and ending at
v_{-1}
and
v_{0}
, or to restrict the set of arrows of
Q_{0}
to those starting and ending at
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
. In other words,
K_{3}
is in two possible ways a subquiver of
Q_{0}
.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
\sigma
be a Bridgeland stability condition on the derived category of representations of
K_{3}
, of heart the category of representations of
K_{3}
. If
V
is a
\sigma
-semistable representation of
K_{3}
of dimension

(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
n_{1}\geqslant 1
, then we have

n_{2}\leqslant 3n_{1}\,.

Proof.

If the moduli space of
\sigma
-semistable representations of
K_{3}
of dimension
(n_{1},n_{2})
is nonempty, then
(e.g. see
[
Kin94
]
) this moduli space has dimension

3n_{1}n_{2}-n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}+1
. In particular, we have
3n_{1}n_{2}-n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}+1\geqslant 0
,
so for
n_{1}\geqslant 1
, we have

n_{2}(3n_{1}-n_{2})\geqslant n_{1}^{2}-1\geqslant 0
, so as
n_{2}\geqslant 0
, we have

3n_{1}\geqslant n_{2}
.
∎

We end this section by a review of a natural operation on stability conditions that will be useful in

section

4.4
.
It is a particular case of a more general action of
\tilde{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
on spaces of stability conditions, see

[
Bri07
, Lemma 8.2]
.
For every
0<\phi<1
and
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, we can construct a new stability condition

\sigma[\phi]\coloneq(Z[\phi],\mathcal{A}[Z,\phi])

on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we define

Z[\phi]_{\gamma}\coloneq e^{-i\pi\phi}Z_{\gamma}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}

be the subcategory of

\mathcal{A}
generated (by extensions)
by the
\sigma
-semistable objects
E
with
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z(E)>\phi
,
and let

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{A}

generated (by extensions) by the

\sigma
-semistable objects
E
with
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z(E)\leqslant\phi
.
Then, denoting
\mathcal{H}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}
the cohomology functors with respect to the bounded

t
-structure of heart
\mathcal{A}
,

\mathcal{A}[Z,\phi]

is the subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of objects
E
such that

\mathcal{H}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
for
i\neq-1,0
,

\mathcal{H}^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
is an object of

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
, and
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\mathcal{A}}(E)
is an object of
\mathcal{Q}_{\phi}
.

For every
0<\phi<1
and
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})

a stability condition on
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
,
moduli spaces of
\sigma
-semistable (resp.
\sigma
-stable) objects coincide with moduli spaces of
\sigma[\phi]
-semistable
(resp.
\sigma[\phi]
-stable) objects.
More precisely:

•

\sigma
-semistable (resp.
\sigma
-stable) objects with

\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}=\psi>\phi
are identified with
\sigma[\phi]
-semistable
(resp.
\sigma[\phi]
-stable) objects with
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z[\phi]=\psi-\phi
.

•

\sigma
-semistable (resp.
\sigma
-stable) objects with

\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z=\psi\leqslant\phi
are identified with
\sigma[\phi]
-semistable (resp.
\sigma[\phi]
-stable) objects with

\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z[\phi]=1+(\psi-\phi)
via
E\mapsto E[1]
.

4.4.
Proof of Theorem
4.2.1

In this section we end the proof of Theorem

4.2.1
, that is, that the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
coincide in restriction to
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
We will use the notions and notation introduced in the previous
section

4.3
.

For every
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
such that

-1<x<1
, it follows from Definition

2.2.8
that
\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]
and

\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to the heart

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
of the stability condition defined by
\sigma
.

As
\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))=(1,-1,0)
,

\gamma(\mathcal{O})=(1,0,1)
, and

\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))=(1,1,3)
, we have by Definition
2.2.10
, for every

(x,y)\in U
:

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))}^{(x,y)}=y-x-\frac{1}{2}-i(x+1)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}^{(x,y)}=y-ix\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}^{(x,y)}=y+x-\frac{1}{2}-i(x-1)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is empty in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
.
In other words, the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
coincide in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be a point in the interior of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
.
Then
\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]
and
\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, with

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])}=-\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))}>0\,,

and

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}<0\,.

We also have

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}>0\,,

and, depending if
x\geqslant 0
or

x<0
,
\mathcal{O}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
or

\mathcal{O}[1]
belongs to

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
.
In any case, the objects
\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,
\mathcal{O}[1]
, and
\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},1/2]
, and so

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},1/2]=\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
On the other hand, we have

\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2])}^{\sigma}<0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}^{\sigma}<0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}^{\sigma}<0\,.

As
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is a category of quiver representations, with simple objects

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,
\mathcal{O}[1]
and
\mathcal{O}(1)
, we have that, for every
E
nonzero object of

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},1/2]
, the central charge

Z^{\sigma}[1/2](E)
is contained in the cone of linear combinations with
nonnegative coefficients of

Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[2])}
,

Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}
,

Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}
.
As
Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma}=-iZ^{\sigma}_{\gamma}
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we have

\operatorname{Im}\,Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[2])}>0\,,\operatorname{Im}\,Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}>0\,,\operatorname{Im}\,Z^{\sigma}[1/2]_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}>0\,.

and so
we conclude that

\operatorname{Im}\,Z^{\sigma}[1/2](E)>0\,.

Thus, if
E
is a
\sigma
-semistable object of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, then

•

either,
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}(E)>\frac{1}{2}
, and so

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)<0
,

•

or,
\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}(E)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, and so
E[1]

is a
\sigma[1/2]
-semistable object
of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\frac{1}{2}]
, so

\operatorname{Im}\,Z^{\sigma}[1/2](E[1])>0
, and so

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)>0
.

In any case, we have
\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)\neq 0
. It follows that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
, restricted to the interior of

\bar{U}_{0,T}^{\mathrm{in}}
, is empty, as

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Figure: an example of configuration of central charges for
\sigma=(x,y)
in the interior of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
with
x<0
. If
E
is a
\sigma
-semistable object of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, then
Z^{\sigma}(E)
belongs to the dotted region.

∎

Lemma 4.4.2
.

We have
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

in restriction to the boundary of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma

4.4.1
.
Let
\sigma
be a point in the interior of the boundary of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
: we have
\sigma\in\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))}=0\}
, or

\sigma\in\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}=0\}
, or

\sigma\in\{\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}=0\}
.
We apply a limit version of the argument for

\sigma
in the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}

given in the proof of Lemma

4.4.1
.
There exists

\epsilon>0
such that

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon]=\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and one shows that the only
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are positive multiple of

\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])
, or
\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))
,
or
\gamma(\mathcal{O})
, or
\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])
.
In each case, the corresponding moduli space of stable objects is a moduli space
of representations of
Q_{0}
of dimension vector
(n,0,0)
, or
(0,n,0)
, or

(0,0,n)
, so is empty if
n>1

(a representation of dimension
(n,0,0)
is
necessarily the direct sum of
n
copies of the simple representation of dimension
(1,0,0)
, and so cannot be stable if
n>1
), and is a point if
n=1
.
So, using Definition

2.5.4

and Definition
1.4.6
,
the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
coincide in restriction to the boundary of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.3
.

The scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is empty in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
and in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.
In other words, we have

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
and in restriction to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma=(x,y)
be a point in the interior of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
.
Then
\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]
,
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\mathcal{O}[1]

belong to
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, with

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])}>0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}<0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}>0\,.

We claim that

\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}>\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])}
.
Indeed, using
y<0
,
x>0
,

-y+x+\frac{1}{2}>0
,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[1]}^{(x,y)}=-y+x+\frac{1}{2}+i(x+1)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}^{(x,y)}=-y+ix\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

this inequality is equivalent to

\frac{x}{-y}>\frac{x+1}{-y+x+\frac{1}{2}}\,,

that is,

y>-x^{2}-\frac{x}{2}\,.

But as
(x,y)
is in the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
, we have
y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, and
x>0
, so
-\frac{x^{2}}{2}>-x^{2}-\frac{x}{2}
.

It follows from the previous inequalities that, denoting
\phi\coloneq\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])}
, the objects

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,
\mathcal{O}[1]
, and
\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\phi]
, and so

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\phi]=\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
In terms of quiver representations,
\mathcal{O}(1)
correspond to the simple
representation
S_{1}
of
Q_{0}
, which is a subrepresentation of every representation of
Q_{0}
of dimension
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with

n_{3}\geqslant 1
. So if
V
is a stable representation of
Q_{0}
of dimension

(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with
n_{3}\geqslant 1
, then
\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}[\phi](V)\geqslant\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}[\phi](\mathcal{O}(1))
.

If
V
is a stable representation of

Q_{0}
of dimension
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with

n_{3}=0
and
n_{1}\neq 0
, it follows from Lemma
4.3.2
that
n_{2}\leqslant 3n_{1}
.
For every nonnegative integers
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
with
n_{1}\neq 0
and
n_{2}\leqslant 3n_{1}
, we have

n_{1}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[2])}+n_{2}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}\leqslant n_{1}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[2])}+3n_{1}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}

\leqslant n_{1}(y-x-\frac{1}{2}-3y)=n_{1}(-2y-x-\frac{1}{2})\,,

and, for
0<x<1
and
y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
,

-2y-x-\frac{1}{2}<x^{2}-x-\frac{1}{2}=(x-\frac{1}{2})^{2}-\frac{3}{4}<-\frac{1}{2}<0\,.

It follows that, for every
E
nonzero
\sigma
-stable object of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, we have either
\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}(E)\leqslant\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[1]}
and so

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)>0
, or
\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)<0
. In particular,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)\neq 0
.

Thus, the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
restricted to the interior of

\bar{U}_{0,R}^{\mathrm{in}}
is empty, as

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Figure: an example of configuration of central charges for

\sigma=(x,y)
in the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
.
If
E
is a
\sigma
-stable object of

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, then

Z^{\sigma}(E)
belongs to the dotted region (it is indeed possible to show that

\operatorname{Arg}(Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[2])}+3Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])})>\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}
).

The proof for
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
instead of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
is completely analogous.
Let
\sigma=(x,y)
be a point in the interior of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.
Then
\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]
,
\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\mathcal{O}

belongs to
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, with

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])}>0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}<0\,,\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}<0\,.

We claim that
\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}>\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}
.
Indeed, using
y<0
,
x<0
,
-y-x+\frac{1}{2}>0
,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}^{(x,y)}=y-ix\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

Z_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}^{(x,y)}=y+x-\frac{1}{2}-i(x-1)\sqrt{x^{2}+2y}\,,

this inequality is equivalent to

\frac{-x}{-y}>\frac{1-x}{-y-x+\frac{1}{2}}\,,

that is,

y>-x^{2}+\frac{x}{2}\,.

But as
(x,y)
is in the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
, we have

y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, and
x<0
, so

-\frac{x^{2}}{2}>-x^{2}+\frac{x}{2}
.

It follows from the previous inequalities
that, denoting
\phi\coloneq\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O})}
, the objects
\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
,

\mathcal{O}[1]
, and
\mathcal{O}(1)
belong to

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\phi]
, and so

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}[Z^{\sigma},\phi]=\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
In terms of quiver representations,

\mathcal{O}(-1)[2]
correspond to the simple representation
S_{-1}
of

Q_{0}
, which is a representation quotient of every representation of
Q_{0}
of dimension
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with
n_{1}\geqslant 1
.
So, if
V
is a stable representation of
Q_{0}
of dimension
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with
n_{1}\geqslant 1
, then

\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}[\phi](\mathcal{O}(-1)[2])\geqslant\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}[\phi](V)
.

If
V
is a stable representation of
Q_{0}
of dimension

(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with
n_{1}=0
and

n_{3}\neq 0
, it follows from
Lemma
4.3.2
that

n_{2}\leqslant 3n_{3}
.
For every nonnegative integers
n_{2}
,
n_{3}

with
n_{3}\neq 0
and
n_{2}\leqslant 3n_{3}
, we have

n_{2}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}+n_{3}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}\leqslant 3n_{3}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])}+n_{3}\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}

\leqslant n_{3}(-3y+y+x-\frac{1}{2})=n_{3}(-2y+x-\frac{1}{2})\,,

and, for
-1<x<0
and
y>-\frac{x^{2}}{2}
,

-2y+x-\frac{1}{2}<x^{2}+x-\frac{1}{2}=(x+\frac{1}{2})^{2}-\frac{3}{4}<-\frac{1}{2}<0\,.

It follows that, for every
E
nonzero
\sigma
-stable object of
\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, we have either
\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}(E)\leqslant\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1))[1]}
and so
\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)>0
, or
\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)<0
. In particular,

\operatorname{Re}\,Z^{\sigma}(E)\neq 0
.

Thus, the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
restricted to the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
is empty, as

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Figure: an example of configuration of central charges for

\sigma=(x,y)
in the interior of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.
If
E
is a
\sigma
-stable object of

\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}
, then

Z^{\sigma}(E)
belongs to the dotted region (it is indeed possible to show that

\operatorname{Arg}Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2])}>\operatorname{Arg}(Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))}+3Z^{\sigma}_{\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])})
).

∎

Lemma 4.4.4
.

We have
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in restriction to the boundary of
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
and in restriction to the boundary of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.

Proof.

Lemma
4.4.4
is a limiting case of Lemma

4.4.3
in the same way as
Lemma
4.4.2

is a limiting case of Lemma

4.4.2
.

On the boundary
|\mathfrak{d}_{0}^{+}|

(resp.
|\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{-}|
)
of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
,
the only
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are positive multiple of

\gamma(\mathcal{O}[1])
(resp.
\gamma(\mathcal{O}(1))
).
The corresponding moduli space of stable objects is a moduli space
of representations of
Q_{0}
of dimension vector
(0,n,0)
(resp.
(0,0,n)
), so is empty if
n>1

(a representation of dimension
(0,n,0)
is
necessarily the direct sum of
n
copies of the simple representation of dimension
(0,1,0)
, and so cannot be stable if
n>1
), and is a point if
n=1
.
So, using Definition

2.5.4

and Definition
1.4.6
,
the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
coincide in restriction to the boundary of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}
.

Similarly, on the boundary
|\mathfrak{d}_{0}^{-}|

(resp.
|\mathfrak{d}_{-1}^{+}|
)
of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
,
the only
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
Z_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\in i\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are positive multiple of

\gamma(\mathcal{O})
(resp.
\gamma(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])
).
The corresponding moduli space of stable objects is a moduli space
of representations of
Q_{0}
of dimension vector
(0,n,0)
(resp.
(n,0,0)
), so is empty if
n>1
, and is a point if
n=1
.
So, using Definition

2.5.4

and Definition
1.4.6
,
the scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
coincide in restriction to the boundary of

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}
.
∎

We can now end the proof of Theorem

4.2.1
: the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
and

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
coincide in restriction to

\bar{U}_{0,T}^{\mathrm{in}}
according to Lemma

4.4.1
and Lemma

4.4.2
,
in restriction to

\bar{U}_{0,L}^{\mathrm{in}}
and

\bar{U}_{0,R}^{\mathrm{in}}

according to Lemma

4.4.3
and Lemma

4.4.4
, and so, as

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0}=\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,T}\cup\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,L}\cup\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0,R}\,,

in restriction to
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0}
, and so,
using the action of

\psi(1)
, in restriction to
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

5.
The equality
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

In
section

5.1
we prove that a consistent scattering diagram which in some sense has the same initial data as
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
in fact coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
.
In

section

5.2
we use
Theorem
3.1.1
and Theorem

4.2.1
to show that

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
satisfies this criterion, and so we get our first main result, Theorem
5.2.1
,
stated as Theorem
0.1.1
in the introduction, that is
the equality
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
.

5.1.
Criterion for
\mathfrak{D}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

Recall that we introduced in

section

1.3
the skew-symmetric form
\langle-,-\rangle
on
M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\langle(a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle=3(a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime})\,.

In
section

1.4
we defined a scattering diagram

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.
In this section we prove
Proposition
5.1.8
, which gives a sufficient criterion to prove that a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
on
U

for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, and let
\mathfrak{d}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
be two rays of

\mathfrak{D}
. The ray
\mathfrak{d}
is a
parent
of
the ray
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
if:

•

\mathfrak{d}
is bounded, that is, we have

|\mathfrak{d}|=\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})-[0,T_{\mathfrak{d}}]m_{\mathfrak{d}}
for some

T_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

•

the endpoint of
\mathfrak{d}
coincides with the initial point
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})
of

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
.

•

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
defines an outgoing ray of the local scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}
.

•

\mathfrak{d}
defines an ingoing ray of the
local scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}
.

•

\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})\leqslant\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})
.

It follows from condition (2) in the Definition

1.2.7
of a scattering diagram that a given ray
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
has finitely many parents.

Definition 5.1.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, and let
\mathfrak{d}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
be two rays of

\mathfrak{D}
. The ray
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a
descendant
of the ray

\mathfrak{d}
of level

\leqslant N
if there exists an integer
N\geqslant 1
, and rays
\mathfrak{d}_{j}
of
\mathfrak{D}
,

0\leqslant j\leqslant N
, such that:

•

\mathfrak{d}_{0}=\mathfrak{d}
.

•

\mathfrak{d}_{N}=\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}

•

For every
0\leqslant j\leqslant N-1
, the ray
\mathfrak{d}_{j}
is a parent of the ray
\mathfrak{d}_{j+1}
.

Definition 5.1.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, and let

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
be a ray of
\mathfrak{D}
.
The ray

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a descendant of level

\leqslant N
if there exists a ray

\mathfrak{d}
of
\mathfrak{D}
such that
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{d}
of level

\leqslant N
.

Definition 5.1.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram on

U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, and let
\mathfrak{d}
be a ray of

\mathfrak{D}
. A ray
\mathfrak{d}
of

\mathfrak{D}
is an
absolute ancestor
if
\mathfrak{d}
has no parent.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram
on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, and
let
\mathfrak{d}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
be two rays of
\mathfrak{D}

such that
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{d}
.
Then, writing
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})=(x,y)
and

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
,
we have

|x^{\prime}-x|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})\,.

Proof.

It is a consequence of Definition

5.1.2
and Lemma
1.2.5
.
∎

Lemma 5.1.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram on

U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
such that

\mathfrak{D}
coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in restriction to

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and let

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
be a ray of
\mathfrak{D}
. Then there exists
a ray
\mathfrak{d}
of
\mathfrak{D}
, which is an absolute ancestor, such that

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

Writing
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})=(x_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}},y_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})
,
we denote

\bar{U}_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}\coloneq\{(x,y)\in\bar{U}\,|x^{2}+2y\leqslant x_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}^{2}+2y_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}\,,|x-x_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})\}\,.

According to Lemma
1.3.2
and
Lemma
5.1.5
, if
\mathfrak{d}
is a ray of

\mathfrak{D}
such that
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is a descendant of

\mathfrak{d}
, then we have

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in\bar{U}_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}
.

We denote

K_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}\coloneq\{(x,y)\in\bar{U}\,|\,\frac{1}{4}\leqslant x^{2}+2y\leqslant x_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}^{2}+2y_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}\,,|x-x_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})\}\,.

K_{\mathfrak{d}}
is a compact subset of
U
and we have
\bar{U}_{\mathfrak{d}}-K_{\mathfrak{d}}\subset\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

As
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent, every ray of

\mathfrak{D}
is either an absolute ancestor or
is the descendant of some other ray.
So, if we assume by contradiction that

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
is not the descendant of an absolute ancestor, we can find an infinite sequence of rays

\mathfrak{d}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\mathfrak{d}_{0}=\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
, and such that, for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{j+1}
is a parent
of
\mathfrak{d}_{j}
. If there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{j})\in\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
,
then we get a contradiction as by assumption
\mathfrak{D}
coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
and every ray of

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
is a descendant of an absolute ancestor. If not, then we have

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{j})\in K_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}
for every

j\in\mathbb{N}
, and as
\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}_{j})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})\leqslant\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})

(by Definition
5.1.2
and Lemma
1.2.5
) for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we get a contradiction with condition (3) of Definition
1.2.7
of a scattering diagram.
∎

Lemma 5.1.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
. Then, every absolute ancestor ray of
\mathfrak{D}
intersects
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{d}
be an absolute ancestor ray of

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
. We claim that
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Indeed, assume by contraction that
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\notin\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Then we have in particular
\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})\in U
and consistency
of
\mathfrak{D}
at the point

\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})
contradicts the assumption that
\mathfrak{d}
is an absolute ancestor ray.
∎

Proposition 5.1.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
which coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

in restriction to
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Then, we have

\mathfrak{D}=S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
.

Proof.

As
\mathfrak{D}
coincides with

S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
in restriction to

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and as every absolute ancestor ray of

\mathfrak{D}
intersects
\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}

by Lemma
5.1.7
,
the absolute ancestor rays
of
\mathfrak{D}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
coincide.
As
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent, it follows by induction on
N
from the uniqueness of local consistent completions given by
Proposition
1.1.9
and from the normalization condition given by condition (1) of Definition

1.2.7

that
\mathfrak{D}
and
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}})
have the same
descendant rays of level
\leqslant N
for every
N\geqslant 1
. This is enough to conclude as every ray of
\mathfrak{D}
is a descendant by Lemma

5.1.6
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of the main result

In
section

1.4
we have defined
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
a scattering diagram
on
U
for
(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, in a completely algorithmic way: as consistent completion of an explicitly given initial scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
.
In
section

2.5
we have defined

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
, a scattering diagram on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
, in terms of intersection Hodge polynomials
of moduli spaces of Bridgeland semistable objects in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.
Our main result, stated as Theorem

0.1.1
in the Introduction, is that these two scattering diagrams coincide:

Theorem 5.2.1
.

We have the equality

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})

of scattering diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{u,v})
.

Proof.

According to Proposition
5.1.8
, it is enough to show that the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
is consistent and coincides with

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in restriction to

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is consistent by Theorem

3.1.1

and coincides with
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in
restriction to

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
by Theorem
4.2.1
.
∎

Theorem 5.2.2
.

We have the equality

\mathfrak{D}_{q^{-}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})

of scattering diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}})
.
Similarly, we have the equality

\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})

of scattering diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{-}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
5.2.1
by the specialization
u=v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
and the semiclassical limit
q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1
.
∎

Theorem 5.2.3
.

We have the equality

\mathfrak{D}_{q^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}})

of scattering diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}})
.
Similarly, we have the equality

\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})

of scattering diagrams on
U
for

(M,\langle-,-\rangle,\mathfrak{g}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})
.

Proof.

It will follow from Theorem
5.2.2
. We have to compare the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}
and

\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}}
. The Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}_{q,-}
is the commutator of the associative algebra

A_{q^{-}}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
, where

z^{m}\cdot z^{m^{\prime}}\coloneq(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}q^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}z^{m+m^{\prime}}
. Similarly, the
Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}_{q,-}
is the commutator of the associative algebra

A_{q^{+}}=\bigoplus_{m\in M}\mathbb{Q}(q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})
, where

z^{m}\cdot z^{m^{\prime}}\coloneq q^{\frac{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}{2}}z^{m+m^{\prime}}
.

We consider
\sigma\colon M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mapsto\{\pm 1\}
,
(a,b)\mapsto(-1)^{ab+a+b}
.
One checks easily (or see

[
Bou18
, Lemma 8.3]
) that

\sigma
is a quadratic refinement, in the sense that

\sigma(m+m^{\prime})=(-1)^{\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle}\sigma(m)\sigma(m^{\prime})

for every
m,m^{\prime}\in M
.
It follows that
F_{\sigma}\colon A_{q^{-}}\mapsto A_{q^{+}}
,

z^{m}\mapsto\sigma(m)z^{m}
is an isomorphism of associative algebras.
If
\mathfrak{D}
is a scattering diagram for

\mathfrak{g}_{q^{-}}
, of rays
\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,H_{\mathfrak{d}})
, we denote
F_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{D})
the scattering diagram for
\mathfrak{g}_{q^{+}}
of rays

\mathfrak{d}=(|\mathfrak{d}|,F_{\sigma}(H_{\mathfrak{d}}))
. As
F_{\sigma}
is an isomorphism,
F_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{D})
is consistent if and only if
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent.

From the explicit description of the scattering diagrams

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}}
in Definitions
1.4.7
and

1.4.8
, we have

F_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}}

(indeed
\sigma(\ell m_{n}^{+})=\sigma(\ell m_{n}^{-})=(-1)^{\ell(n+n+1)}=(-1)^{\ell}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and

\ell\geqslant 1
).
By uniqueness of the consistent completion given by Proposition
1.3.8
, we deduce that
F_{\sigma}(S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}}))=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}})
.

On the other hand, we claim that

F_{\sigma}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{q^{-}})=\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{q^{+}}
. From the explicit description given by Definition

2.5.7
,
it is enough to show that, for every

\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

(-1)^{(\gamma,\gamma)}=\sigma(m_{\gamma})
.
But for
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)
, we have by Definition
2.1.1
,

(\gamma,\gamma)=-3dr-r^{2}-d^{2}+2r\chi
, so

(-1)^{(\gamma,\gamma)}=(-1)^{dr+r+d}
, and as
m_{\gamma}=(r,-d)

by Definition
2.5.3
, we also have
\sigma(m_{\gamma})=(-1)^{dr+r+d}
.

Thus, we get the equality
\mathfrak{D}_{q^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{+}})
by applying
F_{\sigma}
to the equality

\mathfrak{D}_{q^{-}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q^{-}})

given by Theorem
5.2.2
. The equality

\mathfrak{D}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\operatorname{cl}^{+}})

follows by taking the semiclassical limit

q^{\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow 1
.
∎

6.
Applications to moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves

In
section

6.1
we show how Theorem
5.2.1
can be used to obtain an algorithm
computing intersection Hodge numbers of moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}
of Gieseker semistable sheaves.
As an application, we prove Theorem

6.1.2
, stated as Theorem

0.4.1
, that is,
the fact that these intersection Hodge
numbers are concentrated in bidegree
(p,p)
. In
section

6.2
we prove results on the cohomology of the moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}
viewed as real algebraic varieties.
In
section

6.3
we explain that our scattering algorithm leads naturally to a decomposition indexed by trees of the Poincaré polynomial of
M_{\gamma}
and we give some explicit examples.

6.1.
Algorithm

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we denote

M_{\gamma}
the moduli space of
S
-equivalence classes of Gieseker semistable sheaves on

\mathbb{P}^{2}
of class
\gamma
.
The moduli space
M_{\gamma}
is a projective scheme, singular in general, which
can be constructed by geometric invariant theory, see for example
[
HL10
]
.
The moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
of Gieseker stable sheaves is a quasiprojective scheme, open in

M_{\gamma}
. It follows directly from Serre duality and from the definition
of Gieseker semistability that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(E,E)=0
for every Gieseker semistable sheaf
E

with
\gamma(E)\notin\Gamma^{0}
, and so the moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
of stable object is smooth
if
\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
. In fact the moduli space
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
is also smooth if

\gamma\in\Gamma^{0}
, as it is
\mathbb{P}^{2}
if
\gamma=(0,0,1)
, and the emptyset else.

As in
section

2.4
for
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
,
we define intersection Hodge numbers
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})
,
intersection Betti numbers
Ib_{k}(M_{\gamma})
, and intersection Euler characteristics

Ie^{+}(M_{\gamma})
. If
M_{\gamma}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
, which happens for example if

\gamma
is a primitive element of the lattice
\Gamma
, then
M_{\gamma}

is a smooth projective varieties and the intersection Hodge numbers, Betti numbers,
Euler characteristics, are the usual Hodge numbers, Betti numbers, Euler characteristics
of
M_{\gamma}
.

The following Lemma
gives the relation between the
moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}
of Bridgeland-semistable objects
and the moduli spaces
M_{\gamma}
of Gieseker semistable
sheaves. It is a precise version of the general idea that
the notion of stability in the sense of Gieseker can be recover as an asymptotic version
of Bridgeland stability condition.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

We fix some
\gamma=(r,d,\chi)\in\Gamma

such that
r\geqslant 0
.
Then:

•

If
r=0
and
d=0
, we have
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{\gamma}

and
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
for every
\sigma\in U
.

•

If
r=0
and
d>0
, we have
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{\gamma}
and
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
for
\sigma=(x,y)\in U
with
y
large enough.

•

If
r>0
, we have

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{\gamma}
and
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
for

\sigma=(x,y)\in U
,

x<\frac{d}{r}
and
y
large enough.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
ABCH13
, Proposition 6.2]
.
∎

We now explain how Theorem
5.2.1
gives an algorithm to compute the intersection Hodge numbers
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})
.

We fix
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
If
\gamma=(0,0,1)
, then
M_{\gamma}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}=\mathbb{P}^{2}

so
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})=h^{p,q}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{0}

and
\gamma\neq(0,0,1)
, then
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
is empty so
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})=0
, for every
p
and
q
.

So we can assume that
\gamma\notin\Gamma^{0}
. In such case, it follows from the explicit description of
L_{\gamma}
given in
section

2.5

and from Lemma
6.1.1
that

L_{\gamma}
intersects the region in
U
where
M_{\gamma}^{\sigma}=M_{\gamma}
,

M_{\gamma}^{\sigma-\mathrm{st}}=M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
, and so
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma}^{\sigma})=Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})
.
If
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
is empty, then
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})=0
for every
p
and
q
.
So we can assume that
M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
is nonempty. In such case,
Ih^{0,0}(M_{\gamma})\neq 0
, and so, for the unique
j\in J_{\gamma}
such that
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
is a half-line, we have
Ih_{\gamma,j}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})=Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Then, by condition (1) of
Definition
1.2.7
,
(\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j},H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}})
is the unique ray in
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
of support
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}

and of class
m_{\gamma}
.
By Definition
2.5.5
, we have

H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma,j}}\coloneq\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}\\
\gamma=\ell\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{\ell}{2}},v^{\frac{\ell}{2}})}{(uv)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}-(uv)^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}\right)z^{m_{\gamma}}\,.

So, after having determined by induction the
Ih_{\gamma^{\prime},j}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
for
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\gamma}
,
\gamma^{\prime}\neq\gamma
, we can read off

Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})=Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})

from
H_{\mathfrak{d}_{\gamma},j}
.

Thus, we can read the symmetrized Hodge
polynomials
Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})

from
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}
.
But according to Theorem

5.2.1
, we have

\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
. As

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
can be computed in an algorithmic way (see the remark ending

section

1.3
), it is also the case
for
Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

In order to get an effective algorithm, we have to know, for a given
\gamma\in\Gamma
, how to bound the number of steps necessary in the construction of
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})

to compute

Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
. Li-Zhao
[
LZ19a
]
have given an algorithm to compute the actual walls for

\gamma
, and from there, we can get a bound on the value
x^{2}+2y
for the initial point
(x,y)
of the half-line
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
.
Using Lemma
5.1.5
, we get a bound on the
x
-coordinates of the relevant initial rays of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
. Thus, we can algorithmically compute a compact set
K_{\gamma}
in
\bar{U}
such that
Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})
is computed by the restriction of

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
to
K_{\gamma}
.
On the other hand, by combination of
Lemma
1.1.10
and Lemma
1.2.5
, we know that

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})
is increasing when moving from the initial rays by successive local scatterings. Thus, we can bound
\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})
for
\mathfrak{d}
a ray contributing by successive scatterings to the formation of
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
, and for
\sigma\in|\mathfrak{d}|
, by

\varphi_{(x,y)}(m_{\gamma})
.
By condition (3) of Definition
1.2.7
, and the description of
