S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
in restriction to

\bar{U}^{\mathrm{in}}
at the beginning of

section

4.2
, the restriction of
S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
to

K_{\gamma}
contains finitely many such rays . By Lemma
1.1.10
, we know when they are exhausted: when all the newly added rays have
\varphi_{\operatorname{Init}(\mathfrak{d})}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})>\varphi_{(x,y)}(m_{\gamma})
.

We can now prove that, for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, the intersection cohomology of
M_{\gamma}
is concentrated in Hodge bidegrees
(p,p)
, that is Theorem

0.4.1
of the introduction.

Theorem 6.1.2
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have
Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})=0
if
p\neq q
.

Proof.

As we explained above how to compute
the intersection Hodge
polynomials

Ih_{\gamma}(u^{\frac{1}{2}},v^{\frac{1}{2}})=(-(uv)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-\dim M_{\gamma}}\sum_{p,q=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}}(-1)^{p+q}Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})u^{p}v^{q}

from the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
, the result follows from Corollary

1.4.12
. In short, the result is true for the initial rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
(the corresponding moduli spaces are points), and then propagates by wall-crossing.
∎

As we recalled in the Introduction, Theorem
6.1.2
is well-known

[
ESm93
,
Bea95
,
Mar07
]
if
\gamma
is primitive: in this case, semistability coincides with stability,

M_{\gamma}
is smooth, intersection cohomology coincides with ordinary cohomology, and one can show that the cohomology ring is generated by the Künneth components of the Chern classes of the universal sheaf. In general,
M_{\gamma}
is singular and Theorem
6.1.2

is much less clear. In fact, Theorem

6.1.2
does not seem to appear previously in the
literature,
even if, with the extra assumption
r>0
, a different proof can be obtained from the results proved in
[
MM18
]
.

Theorem
6.1.2
implies immediately the following Corollary

6.1.3
.

Corollary 6.1.3
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have:

•

Ib_{2k+1}(M_{\gamma})=0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Ie^{+}(M_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{N}
, and, if

M_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{st}}
is nonempty,
Ie^{+}(M_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
.

6.2.
Real algebraic geometry

We now discuss an application to the real algebraic geometry of the moduli spaces

M_{\gamma}
.
We equip
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with its natural real structure whose real locus is the real projective plane
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. As the definition of Gieseker semistable sheaves
makes sense over essentially any base, it follows that for every

\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, the moduli space
M_{\gamma}
has a natural real structure and we denote
M_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R})
its real locus.
In particular, there is a natural action of
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{Z}/2
on the intersection cohomology groups
IH^{k}(M_{\gamma},\mathbb{Q})
.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and for every
0\leqslant p\leqslant\dim M_{\gamma}
, the complex conjugation in

\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
acts as
(-1)^{p}

on
IH^{2p}(M_{\gamma},\mathbb{Q})
.

Proof.

In all the arguments used in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
, we can replace the intersection Hodge
polynomials valued in
\mathbb{Z}[u,v]
by the class of the intersection cohomology in the Grothendieck group of the category of
mixed Hodge structures with action of

\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
. In all the explicit formulas defining

S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
, we only have to replace
uv
by

H^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{Q})
. The analogue of Theorem
5.2.1
is then an equality of scattering diagrams with values in the Grothendieck group of the category of
mixed Hodge structures with action of

\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
. The result then follows from the fact that the complex conjugation in
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
acts as
-1
on
H^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{Q})
. In short, the logic is identical to the one used to prove Theorem
6.1.2
: the result is true for the initial rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}

(the corresponding moduli spaces are points), and then propagates by wall-crossing.
∎

If
\gamma
is primitive, then

M_{\gamma}
is smooth, so
M_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R})
is a compact manifold. We denote

e(M_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}))
its topological Euler
characteristic.

Corollary 6.2.2
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
primitive, we have

e(M_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}))=\sum_{p=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}}(-1)^{p}b_{2p}(M_{\gamma})=\sum_{p=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}}(-1)^{p}h^{p,p}(M_{\gamma})\,.

Proof.

For
\gamma
primitive,
M_{\gamma}
is smooth, intersection cohomology coincides with usual cohomology, and so the result follows from Theorem
6.2.1

by the Lefschetz fixed point formula applied to the complex conjugation.
∎

It is possible to give a simpler proof of Corollary
6.2.2
.
According to
[
ESm93
,
Bea95
,
Mar07
]
if
\gamma
is primitive, then the cohomology ring of
M_{\gamma}
is generated by Künneth components of the Chern classes of the universal sheaf on

M_{\gamma}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
. So the result follows from the fact that complex conjugation acts as
(-1)^{p}
on the

p
-th Chern class of a real sheaf, and as

(-1)^{p}
on
H^{2p}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{Q})
.

6.3.
Tree decompositions

According to Theorem
6.1.2
, the study of the intersection Hodge numbers

Ih^{p,q}(M_{\gamma})
can be reduced to the study of the intersection Betti numbers

Ib_{2k}(M_{\gamma})
.
In particular, the intersection Poincaré polynomial

P(M_{\gamma})\coloneq\sum_{p=0}^{\dim M_{\gamma}}Ib_{2p}(M_{\gamma})q^{p}

has nonnegative coefficients.

The scattering algorithm of
section

6.1
induces a decomposition

P(M_{\gamma})=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}}P_{T}(M_{\gamma})\,,

where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is a set of oriented weighted trees immersed in
\bar{U}
, and where each

P_{T}(M_{\gamma})
is a polynomial in
q
with integer coefficients.
Each
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is contained in the support of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{u,v}=S(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v})
, has roots at some of the initial points
s_{n}
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
, and has a unique unbounded leave coinciding with
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
, where
j
is the unique
j\in J_{\gamma}
such that
\bar{R}_{\gamma,j}
is a half-lines. Each edge of
T
is weighted by some positive integer and oriented in the direction of increasing value of

\varphi_{\sigma}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})
. Each non-root vertex of
T
has finitely many ingoing edges and one outgoing edge, and the tropical balancing condition is satisfied at each such vertex. The only obstruction to the embedding of
T
in
\bar{U}
is the fact that two roots of
T
can map to the same initial point
s_{n}
. It is related to the fact that, in the support of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
, there are two rays coming out from each
s_{n}
, and
T
might contain these two rays.

Indeed, such trees naturally index the various terms obtained by successive local scatterings in the algorithmic construction of
S(\mathfrak{D}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{in}})
. The fact that the contribution of each tree is a polynomial in
q
with integer coefficients follows from the formalism of admissible series of Kontsevich-Soibelman (see
[
KS11
, §6]
). We conjecture that the coefficients of the polynomials
P_{T}(M_{\gamma})
are nonnegative, and that
q^{-(\dim M_{\gamma}-\deg P_{T}(M_{\gamma}))}P_{T}(M_{\gamma})
are unimodular palindromic polynomials.

Even if our result holds only at the numerical level of Betti numbers, we can think of the various terms indexed by
T
as coming from various locally closed strata in
M_{\gamma}
, of codimension
\dim M_{\gamma}-\deg P_{T}(M_{\gamma})
, parametrizing Gieseker semistable sheaves which are obtained from the line bundles

\mathcal{O}(n)
by some precise pattern of exact
triangles in
\operatorname{D}^{b}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
.

This decomposition of
P(M_{\gamma})
seems to be new in general and probably deserves further study.
We make only one observation.
The moduli spaces of Gieseker semistable sheaves supported in dimension
1
, that is with
\gamma=(0,d,\chi)
, have been quite explicitly studied for low values of
d

in

[
DM11
,
Mai11
,
Mai13a
,
CM14
,
CC15
,
BMW14
,
Yua14
]
.
One key aspect of these studies is the construction of explicit locally closed decompositions of the moduli spaces
M_{(0,d,\chi)}
(for low degree,

d\leqslant 6
), whose strata are characterized by the existence of a
resolution by direct sum of line bundles of some numerical type. For
d\leqslant 5
, one can check explicitly that
our decomposition according to the trees
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}

matches the decomposition of
P(M_{\gamma})

induced by these decompositions, and for

d=(6,1)
, it matches the slightly more refined decomposition according to the
first destabilizing wall, see
[
CC15
]
.

A quite simple example: shape of the unique tree
T
contributing
to
\gamma=(0,1,1)
(the precise embedding in
\bar{U}
can be recovered from the explicit description of the initial rays of
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u,v}
and from the tropical balancing condition):

Above the vertex, extensions of
\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]
by
\mathcal{O}
become stable, corresponding to the fact that all the elements of

M_{(0,1,1)}
are of the form
\mathcal{O}_{l}
for
l
a line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and so admit a resolution of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{l}\rightarrow 0\,.

We can check that
P_{T}(M_{(0,1,1)})=1+q+q^{2}
, which is indeed the Poincaré polynomial of

M_{(0,1,1)}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{2}
. More complicated examples are given in the proof of
Theorem
0.4.5
in the following section §
6.4
.

The above trees are essentially identical to the attractor trees discussed in a more general and partially conjectural context in

[
KS14
, §3.2]
. They are of the same nature as the attractor flow trees of the supergravity literature

[
Den00
]
, except that, in the supergravity context, the trees can have root at an attractor point, which is a smooth point of the moduli space, analogue of our
U
, whereas all our trees end at a singular point of the moduli space
(the points
s_{n}
where the central charge of
\mathcal{O}(n)
goes to
0
). The supergravity context should be relevant for compact Calabi-Yau 3-folds, whereas the present paper is about the noncompact Calabi-Yau 3-fold
K_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
.

6.4.
Test of the
\chi
-independence conjecture: proof of Theorem
0.4.5

We prove Theorem
0.4.5
that is, for every
d\leq 4
, the intersection
Poincaré polynomial
P(M_{(0,d,\chi)})
is independent of
\chi
.
In order to save space, we will use Theorem
0.4.4
, proved
in
[
Bou19b
]
, according to which

P(M_{(0,d,\chi)})
only depends on
\chi
through
\gcd(d,\chi)
.

For
d=1
and
d=2
, the moduli space
M_{(0,d,\chi)}
is isomorphic to the linear system of degree
d
curves and so does not depend on
\chi
: there is nothing to prove.

For
d=3
, there are two cases to consider, that we can choose to be
\chi=1

and
\chi=3
, and there is something to prove.

Shape of the unique tree contributing to

\gamma=(0,3,1)
:

T=

The uniqueness of
T
is related to the fact that every sheaf
E
in
M_{(0,3,1)}

admits a resolution of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-2)^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

see
[
DM11
]
.

Denoting
[n]_{q}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q^{k}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can check running the scattering algorithm that

P(M_{(0,3,1)})=P_{T}(M_{(0,3,1)})=[9]_{q}[3]_{q}\,.

Shape of the two trees contributing to
\gamma=(0,3,3)
:

T_{0}=

T_{1}=

The tree
T_{0}
corresponds to the codimension
0
stratum in

M_{(0,3,3)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

and the tree
T_{1}
corresponds to the codimension
1
stratum in
M_{(0,3,3)}

of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-2)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

see
[
DM11
]
. We can check running the scattering algorithm that

P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,3,3)})=[9]_{q}(1+q^{2})\,,

P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,3,3)})=[9]_{q}q\,,

and so

P(M_{(0,3,3)})=P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,3,3)})+P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,3,3)})=[9]_{q}[3]_{q}\,.

In particular, we have indeed
P(M_{(0,3,1)})=P(M_{(0,3,3)})

and this proves Theorem
0.4.5
for
d=3
.

For
d=4
, there are three cases to consider, that we can chose to be

\chi=1
,
\chi=2
, and
\chi=4
.

Shape of the two trees contributing to

\gamma=(0,4,1)
:

T_{0}=

T_{1}=

Using the notation of

[
DM11
, §3]
,
T_{0}
corresponds to the
codimension
0
stratum
X_{0}
in
M_{(0,4,1)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-3)^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

and
T_{1}
corresponds to the codimension
2
stratum
X_{1}
in
M_{(0,4,1)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-3)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,.

We can check running the scattering algorithm that

P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,4,1)})=[12]_{q}(1+q+3q^{2}+3q^{3}+3q^{4}+q^{5}+q^{6})\,,

P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,4,1)})=[12]_{q}[3]_{q}q^{2}\,,

and so

P(M_{(0,4,1)})=P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,4,1)})+P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,4,2)})=[12]_{q}(1+q+4q^{2}+4q^{3}+4q^{4}+q^{5}+q^{6})\,.

The moduli space
M_{(0,4,1)}
is smooth and its Poincaré polynomial has been previously computed by torus localization in
[
CM14
]
.

Shape of the three trees contributing to

\gamma=(0,4,2)
:

T_{0}=

T_{1}=

T_{2}=

Using the notation of

[
DM11
, §4]
,
T_{0}
corresponds to the
codimension
0
stratum
X_{0}
in
M_{(0,4,2)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-2)^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

T_{1}
corresponds to the codimension
1
stratum
X_{1}
in
M_{(0,4,2)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-2)^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

and
T_{2}
corresponds to the codimension
3
stratum
X_{2}
in
M_{(0,4,2)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-3)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

We can check running the scattering algorithm that

P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,4,2)})=[12]_{q}(1+2q^{2}+2q^{4}+q^{6})\,,

P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,4,2)})=[12]_{q}q(1+2q+3q^{2}+2q^{3}+q^{4})\,,

P_{T_{2}}(M_{(0,4,2)})=[12]_{q}q^{3}\,,

and so

P(M_{(0,4,2)})=[12]_{q}(1+q+4q^{2}+4q^{3}+4q^{4}+q^{5}+q^{6})\,.

Shape of the two trees contributing to
\gamma=(0,4,4)
:

T_{0}=

T_{1}=

Using the notation of

[
DM11
, §5]
,
T_{0}
corresponds to the
codimension
0
stratum
X_{0}
in
M_{(0,4,4)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 4}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 4}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,,

and

T_{1}
corresponds to the
codimension
1
stratum
X_{1}
in
M_{(0,4,4)}
of sheaves
E
admitting a resolution
of the form

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-2)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0\,.

We can check running the scattering algorithm that

P_{T_{0}}(M_{(0,4,4)})=[12]_{q}(1+2q^{2}+q^{3}+2q^{4}+q^{6})\,,

P_{T_{1}}(M_{(0,4,4)})=[12]_{q}[3]_{q}^{2}q\,,

and so

P(M_{(0,4,4)})=[12]_{q}(1+q+4q^{2}+4q^{3}+4q^{4}+q^{5}+q^{6})\,.

Comparing the previous results, we obtain that indeed

P(M_{(0,4,1)})=P(M_{(0,4,2)})=P(M_{(0,4,4)})

and this proves Theorem

0.4.5
for
d=4
.
